=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.97)
STATEMENT
On 29 October 45, We, the Representatives of
.CC for Rumania, Colonel I. M. Leontiev and Sn.Sgt.
Rubinstein, of the inistry of Foreign Affairs,
secretary Ionescu, of the Ministry of the Interior,
Police ohief raciun and Commissar A. Margarit,
Opened the sealed rooms in the former Japanese
Embassy at 25 Blvd. Dacia and removed part of the
property as indicuted in the list attached hereto and
sent to the CC Depot. The abovementioned property
was handed over to Jr. Lt. A. N. lujnikov as per list.
The remaining property was sealed in two rooms
under the wax seal of the  Cc for Rumania and placed
under the responsibility of Mr. Jusui.
Various newspupers, clippings and revues were
burned by the Jommission.

=== Page 105 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
January 29, 1946
No. 719
SUBJECT: Report on Japanese Archives and Records
THE HONORABLE
THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
WASHINGTON.
SIR:
With reference to my telegram No. 867 of November
10, 1945, and the Department's Circular Telegram of
November 16, 1945, I have the honor to report that on
January 21, 1946, the Office of the United States
Military Representation, Allied Control Commission for
Rumania, reported that on October 29 and 30, 1945,
representatives of the Allied Control Commission, in-
cluding an American officer, opened the sealed rooms
of the Japanese Embassy and office of the Military
Attache, and confiscated in the name of the ACc the
property found in these rooms. Lists of the confis-
cated property are enclosed.
I have been informed by the Rumanian Foreign Office
that the Japanese Government did not own any real estate
property in Rumania, and that the aforementioned build-
ings in which the offices of the Legation and Military
Attache were located were leased by the Japanese Govern-
ment.
The Department will note that no diplomatic or
consular records were found, In this connection, the
Swedish Minister, who was charged with protection of
Japanese interests in Rumania, in his letter of November
7, 1945, stated that the Japanese archives had been
burned, and that nearly all of the property owned by
the Japanese state was sold by members of the Legation
during the autumn of 1944.
General

Russian authorities that several items such as table-
ware, dishes and table linen had been turned over to
the Japanese officials now being held in the intern-
ment camp at Ghencea, for their personal use.
Respectfully yours,
Burton Y. Berry
Representative of the United States

STATEMENT
On 30 October 1945 We, the Representatives of
the ACC for Rumania, Col. Leontiev, Major Petrov
and Sn. Sgt. Rubinstein, the Representative of the
Ministry of Foreign Affairs, lst Secretary Malderescu,
the Representative of the Ministry of the Interior,
Inspector Nedelcu, and Police Chief Craciun, opened
the sealed rooms in the building at No. 36, Boulevard
Alexandru Constantinescu, occupied by the former
Japanese Military Attache Shimanuki.
The confiscated property found in said rooms
agrees with the list attached hereto and was handed
over to Jr. Lt. Plujnikov for delivery to the ware-
house of the ACC for Rumania.
The personal effects of General Fujicenka found
in the rooms which were unsealed were returned. These
effects consisted of 6 items, while a portion of his
belongings (various small items) which were scattered
around were taken by the former Attache Shimanuki.
Various newspaper clippings, revues, and some old
carbon paper were burned by the Commission.
One 8mm film projection machine for the showing
of films seized at that time was removed, as well as
a portfolio containing various topographical and
geographic maps.
The members of the Commission and the persons
interned made no claims in connection with the proce-
dure of the property removed.
This statement was read and translated from Russian
into Rumanian and Japanese.

AIRGRAM SENT
Department of State
NO. A- 16
Washington,
TIME
1706
AmLegation,
1708
rch 18, 1946
Bangkok.
Reference Department's telegram 13 of Jan 9 and your
14/1-3046
telegram 84 of Jan 30.
In view uniform action being taken throughout world by
China, UK, USSR and US aimed at controlling Japanese public
assets effectively, Department desires you discuss with British
and Chinese Missions Siam establishment committee to take
similar action there in behalf four powers concerned.
When established, committee should discuss matter with
Siamese Foreign Office and, if possible, arrange to accept
custody of all Japanese consular and diplomatic property in
CS/LF
Siam including official funds of mission and consulates and
private funds belonging Japanese diplomatio or consular
officials.
All funds acquired should be deposited in trust account
jointly controlled by diplomatic representatives of China, UK
and US for later disposition.
Dept favors storage of movable official property wherever
practicable. If storage impracticable by reason of excessive
quantity, disproportionate expense, risk of depreciation or
deterioration and the like, official property, other than
archives, may be sold and the proceeds deposited in the trust
account.
Real property may not be sold without reference to
Department but it may be rented and proceeds deposited in trust
account.
The private property of Japanese officials should not be
sold at present.
Withdrawals from the trust account for moving, storage,
rents and custodial expenses in connection with Japanese officiei
property and also, if occasion develops, withdrawals for reliefy
payments through a protecting Power to indigent Japanese nationals,
are considered appropriate by the Department
3-4-46

AIRGRAM SENT
Department of State
NO.
Washington,
TIME
-2-
DEPT will not entertain requests for destruction of any part of
the archives. They must be preserved in so far as possible.
DEPT hopes to be able to obtain employees or teams of examiners
for dispatch to places where they will serve a useful purpose. However,
the actual examination may be delayed for some time unless the mismion
concerned can arrange for examination locally.
It is possible thet representatives of the other three powers will
be in a position to provide staffs for purposes of examination in which
case arrangements should be made for reciprocal exchange of informatiom.
Offer of access and exchange of information should be made in accordance
with the original intention of joint custody by the four powers vieterious
over Japan, the United States, Soviet Union, Great Britain and China.
Where the archives are in the custody of the local sovereign
authority efforts should be made to ensure access to representatives
of the DEPT and to share the information obtained from the documents by
the local authorities.

SECRET
41946
BJECT:
Report regeruing Japanese Diplomatic and Consular
Records, Archives and Property.
DREIGN
CORRELATIGN
THE HONORABLE
THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
HASHINGTON.
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to thé Department's circular
airgram of January 31, 194o regording the Japanese diplo-
894.414/2-646
matic and consular records, arehives and property, end to
submit the following report in this regard.
THE JAPANESE ARCHIVES
A detailed inventory of the Japenese archives in Panamá
was forwarded with the Embessy's despatch No. 2857, October
1, 1945. No further study of these records is contemplated,
although photostatic copies of certain portions of the
arehives have been made and were forworded to the Depart-
ment with the Embassy's desnntch No. 690, Jamuary 25, 1946.
JAPANESE FUNDS
At the time of the surrender of Japan the sum of ap-
proximately 36.00 was turned over by the Swiss represen-
tative in Panam to the Foreign Office of Ponamá. This is
all the official Japenese funds held in this area. In ad-
dition to this, the Alien Property Custodian holds approxi-
mately $190,000.00 in cash and real property valued at
8,000.00 belonging to Japanese individuals end companies
here before the outbreck of the war. Whether any of these
CS/VJ
funds are official Japanese Government funds is not known.
The
SECRET
FILED
C.
f
(D)

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
The largest deposits cre the following: AMANO & Company,
$141,320.04; the Yokohama Specia Bank, $8,087.62; and the
Mitsui Bank Ltd., $5,293.72. The real estate, which con-
sists of Finca No. 89l9 at Rio Abajo near Panamá City, is
of record as belonging to Y. Amano and Yochi ICAWA. An
examination of the report forwarded with the Embessy's
despatch No. 1946, June 21, 1945, wil1 revenl the names
and amounts of the funds involved.
JAPANESE OFFICLAL PROPERTY
A copy of the inventory of the officinl Japanese
property was forwarded to the Department with the Em-
bassy's despatch No. 2456, August 17, 1945. A11 of that
property now is in the custody of the Panamanian Govern-
ment. At the time of the surrender of Japan, the Panama-
nian Government was very interested in procuring the use
of the two Packard automobiles which were part of the
property of the former Japanese Legation. These cars
re now being used by the Ministry of Foreign Relations
of the Republic of Penamá and the Embassy would be very
reluctant to ask for their return, especinlly considering
the most cordial cooperation rendered by the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs in the taking over of the archives of the
former German Legation in May 1945 and of the former
Japanese Legation in August 1945. The Embassy's telegrams
No. 976, November 5, 1945, and No. 1016, November 19, 1945,
indicated that the British and Chinese Ministers and I were
in agreement that the Panammian Government should be given
all the official Japanese property that was left. The Em-
bassy has not yet received cny indication from the Depart-
ment whether this means of disposel of the property wes
satisfactory end if the Department feels that the property
should be taken over by this Embassy, acting on behalf of
Great Britain, China and Soviet Russia, I am sure that the
Panamnian Government will not offer any objection to the
conversion of the property into cash, which together with
the 36.00 which the Panamenian Government already holds,
could be placed in a trust account. It is hard to estimate
what the cale of this furniture and office equiment would
net. There is some silver which might command a good
price, but the furniture end office equipment has not been
well taken care of during the last four years and will
probably not realize much. It is strongly recommend, there
fore, that the Embassy be given cuthorization to inform the
Panamanion Government either that it may keep what remains
of the Japanese official property or that it should sell a
the office equipment nd furniture formerly owned by the
Japanese Legation for whatever price can be otained and
place the funds realized from this sale in a trust account
pending further instructions from the United States, Great
Britá
SECRET

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Britain, China and Soviet Russia. In any event, it is
hoped that the Panamanien Government will be allowed to
keep the two cars that they have been using since
August 1945.
Respectfully yours,
Frank T. Hines

CONVIDENTIAL
(see A B of Creular
NO.
+ 10/23/45)
TheHonorable
Ray Atherton,
American Ambassador,
Ottawa.
Sir:
The Department refers to the Embassy's despatch
no. 3587 of February 11, 1946 concerning the Canadian
Government's assumption of custody of Japanese official
arohives and property in Canada.
As you are aware, ciroular instructions have beon
2-1146
sent to all United States Missions in countries where
the former Japanese Government was officially repre-
sented, requesting that in cooperation with their
appropriate Allied colleague they take over custody of
all official Japanese archives, property and funds.
These instructions were based on the directive issued
by General MacArthur in Ostober 1945 and were intended
to apply uniformly to all Missions concerned. In most
countries the Allied Missions found that the most
expeditious method of thus assuming custody was to
establish a committee, composed of one or more members
from each Mission, which would seek to handle all
problems arising in conneotion with the examination,
storage and disposition of the various types of Japanese
effeots. Naturally, different problems have arisen in
different countries, but in general the following pro-
cedure has been adhered to cohsistently:
1. Archives. All official archives, seals, stamps
eto. of the former Japanese Misslon are being examined
and placed in storage. Sale of axohives has in no
instance been sanctioned. Storage charges have been
met from funds on deposit in a joint trust account, such
funds having been acaumulated either from the taking over
of Japanese balances in local banks or from the sale of
officlal Japanese effects.
2. Official Japanese Property. Wherever possible
storage of such property has been advocated. However in
instances where storage is impraeticable due to excessive
quantity, disproportionate expense, risk of depreciation
or deterioratien and the like, property is sold and, as
stated above, the proceeds deposited in a trust account
jointly controlled by the Committee.
3. Property of Former Japanese Officials. Ahl such
property has been stored pending decision as toits ginal
disposition.
You

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Tou are requested to disouss the foregoing with
Missions at Ottawa with a view to assuming jeint
custody of Japanese official arehives, property and
funds in Canada at an early date. In the event of
any difriculties you should report to the Department
which will issue specifie instructions.
Very truly yours,
For the Secretary of.State:

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch
AIR MAIL
No. 13,477 of Feb. 11, 1946,
American Embassy, Santiago.
COPY OF TRANSLATION
SOME FACTS IN FORMER JAPANESE MINISTER YAMAGATA'S DIARY
1942
April 1. Went to attend the inauguration of President Rios at
Congress with my Secretaries and Military Attaches. The masses
applauded us when we went in. I felt very happy.
April 5. Went to the reception of Argentine Embassy in honor
of Argentine Minister of Foreign Affairs Mr. Guiñazú.
April 7. Went to see off Rear-Admiral Yukishita. Mr. Gon came
to see me, talked about the magazines.
April 8. Mr. Miyasaki and Mr. Nomura came to tell me that three
Japanese had trouble to go to MAHako (?), asked me to help. I
promised them immediately.
Mr. "Ga" came to talk with me about many things. After lunch,
called on Secretary Walf, handed him two personal letters, to ask
Mr. Irie to transfer them.
Our mission invited president and Mrs. Rios to Union Club to dinner
at 9 c'clock.
April 9. Very busy for the publication of naval engagements in
the Indian Ocean.
Went to lunch at Italian Embassy. The German, Swiss and Rumanian
missions were present.
Mr. Obo called on me saying that he will resign his position as
adviser. It was very annoying for me.
Sent cable.
April 10. Mr. "GA" phoned me that Alessandri promised to receive
me at 4 P.M.
By appointment, called on Alessandri at 4 P.M. I advised him to
maintain the peace and to prevent the bankruptey of human civiliza-
tion. He told me as frankly as usual that, they will of course,
keep neutral policy.
Sent cable.
Received cable from Mr. Irie and Mr. Yoshioka, saying that they
will sail at 5 P.M. today.
April 1l. Mr. Huneeus came to tell me that Kuadors' family can
start on first ship, but the nurse is excluded.
Sent cable to report the activities of Americans.
April 12. Began to adjust the war bulletins of Imperial Headquer-
ters, and other propaganda matters.
April 13. Went to Spanish Eabassy.
April 14. Cable came from Gaimusho, saying that Chilean government
had not replied to the congratulations of Japanese Emperor to
President Rios. I was very worried. Mr. Nagamine is ill and cannot
work. So I had to go to Protocolo myself to talk about the matter.
Called on Mr. Huneeus, told him about the cable which came from
Gaimusho. He was extremely happy.
April 15.

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-2
April 15. Mr. Hernández called on me. I thought that he ap-
peared very strange. I had idea he might be a spy.
By appointment, went to see Spanish counselor. It was a cable
that came from Mr. Nayai. It says that he will leave Bogot& 15th.
April 16. Wrote letters to Mr. Huneeus and Mr. GA, sent them
personally.
April 17. Drafted a cable about the Chilean newspaper men.
April 20. Sent the cable about Chilean newspaper men.
r. Huneeus called on me and asked me whether it is all right
or not if he tells Mr. Cecil B. Lyon (an American Secretary)
about the decision of his own wife to take a ship. Of course I
told him that I have no objection.
April 2l. Received Mr. Martinez, talked about the celebration
of Emperor's Birthday.
A certain economist came to see me, and asked me importunately
for money. I was very disturbed. A little later, Mr. Desanti (?)
and other Italians came to see me.
April 22. By request, went to see Spanish counselor. He gave me
a letter from Mr. -.
Went to Ital ian Embassy to talk with Secretary NABALINI (?) about
what Mr. Desanti had said yesterday.
Mr. Takada heard from Banco Central that the Conference of Central
Banks ought to take place at Washington in the next two months,
will discuss the problem of fund freezing.
April 23. Mr. G came to talk with me about many things.
April 25. Vice-Minister received me at 11 A.M., entreated me
about the matter of Chileans in Tokyo returning home.
April 29. Emperor's Birthday.
Presented the Japanese Colony.
Ne had a reception in the afternoon. All the members of German,
Italian and Spanish Embassies were present. Besides, the Argen-
tine Ambassador and Counselor, Consul General of Honduras, many
members of Foreign Office headed by Vice-Minister, Errazuriz,
Valenzuela, González Smith, etc., came also.
April 30. By appointment went to see Mr. Wolf, talked about the
intelligence work. It's a very difficult thing. I am very dis-
appointed.
May lst. Went to German sports Verhein to be present at the Ger-
man National day. It was a great banquet. But most of them were
Axis.
May 4. Mr. Huneeus came, saying that he had called on a Japanese.
He also said that nurse of Kuadors' family can sail with the first
exchange boat. He himself is now writing a book.
May 5. Called onir. Moreno. Sent cable to Gaimusho about the
correspondence from Perå.
May 7. Mr. Okayama came to talk with me about the leaving of
Japanese in Bolivia and Perú.
Mr. GA came to see me at 4 P.M. He is a good fellow. He came
to bring me the magazines at 7 P.M. again.
May 8.

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-3-
May 8. Japanese won at Coral sea engagements.
Mr. Gon came to talk about the information of Peru.
Have got in touch with Military Attaché to watch the actions
of S.S. MICOYEN (?).
May 10. Read Times. The construction of American ships seems
to be behind the times. It's worth while to pay attention.
May ll. Received instruction from Gaimusho. Requested Mr. E
(maybe Errázuriz) to come to talk with me.
May 16. Kawaguchi called on me, talked about the matter of
copper. Mr. E tranmitted to us that the Foroign Minister is not
to be a question, it need not be a worry.
May 18. Interview wi th Mr. Riveros (?) at 5 P.M. At 7 P.M. the
Presiient received me.
May 19. Talked with German Ambassador in the morning. Gen.
Ahumada and Mr. Nieto called on me. Sent a short cable.
May 21. Went to Bank of Chile to draw out money. Wrote the
cable.
May 28. Wish to interview Foreign Minister today, but as I have
not been well prepared, I stopped. Mr. Nieto came to talk about
many things.
May 31. Received a letter from Mr. "GA. NI" (?) was the matter
of Venezuela (?).
June 1. Ordered Mr. Nagamine to go to Foreign Office to arrange
my audience with Foreign Minister and farewell dinner for Mr.
Orrego (?). Called on German Ambassador, talked about armed mer-
chant vessels and the attitude of President regarding neutrality.
Mr. GA came, saying that he had talked with Conservative leaders.
However, the attack on the continent is very difficult.
Mr. Gon called on me, talked about the sessions of Congress, say-
ing that in short, the Fifth Investigation Committee is something
not so bad.
June 2. Asked Nagamine to go to Foreign Office to hasten them
about my audience with Foreign Minister.
June 3. Mr. Nagamine went to Foreign Office to inquire about
the matter of banquet and the declaration of war of Mexico. Sent
cable regarding Mexican Declaration. Interview with Foreign
Minister at 4 P.M. I spoke in English, but I couldn't speak
well. The interview lasted one hour. Wrote a cable.
June 5. Two Socialist leaders spoke in a Socialist meeting, in-
sisted upon the rupture of relations with Axis. Ordered Mr. Naga-
mine to go to Italian Embassy, to consult about the restriction
of American paper money.
Met Labarca's father at 4 p.m., got money, and conversed many
things. Asked the Japanese Commercial Agents to confer with me
about the prohibition of U.S. bank notes.
June 6. The matter of U.S. bank notes is very troublesome. Ob-
serving the instruction of Geimusho, ordered Mr. Nagamine to
confer with Director of Diplomatic Department. Mr. BRUKA (?).
German, came to talk with me about the male of U.S.$5, ooo, this
afternoon.
Labarca's mother phoned Miyasaki that she had agreed already.
Ordered

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-4-
Ordered Mr. Miyasaki to call on Mr. Ovelle.
Sent two cables.
June 8. Mr. Moreno called on me this sfternoon, saying that his
health is very bad.
June 9. Mr. GA came to see me.
June 10. El Siglo published news saying that German and Japanese
diplomats are now acting very severely. Germans are going to
make a protest on that matter. Mr. GA said it's very adequate
to take such a step.
Arranged the letters from Labarca's father, and correspondence
from Perú.
June 1l. Asked Mr. Nagemine to inquire of Mr. D about the situa-
tion of Socialists, and on his way to call on Spanish Embassy to
confer about the matter of money.
I myself called on German and Italian Ambassadors. We all recog-
nized that the situation is worse.
An instruction from Gaimusho wie  s me to go to Argentina, it's
very unreasonable. But it can't a helped, I have to do this.
June 12. Called on Mr. Valenzue. ., Mr. Errazuriz and Mr. Yrarrá-
zaval to ask them about the situation, the best advice given was
from Mr. Errázuriz.
June 13. Through Mr. Miyasaki of Mitsui interviewed Mr. SU (?).
in Italian Counselor Mr. Migone (?) came to inquire of me on be-
half of Mrs. Lyon, about the sailing of her parents (Mrs. Migone
is a friend of Mr. Lyon). I told him the latest news.
June 14. Conversed with Mr. Miyasaki, he has the intention of
going back to B.A.
June 15. Called on Mr. Riveros, inquired about the situation of So-
cialists, entreated him on the matter of money. The local Japan-
ese had contributed a great deal of money already.
June 16. Went to Mr. Riveros' house, it was a splendid one,
inquired about the million dollars. Invited Mr. Ovalle of El
Imparcial to Hotel Carrera to dinner. He also talked about the
matter of million dollars.
June 17. Conversed with Mr. Takada about the money.
Mr. Miyasaki brought me a letter from Mr. Zúñiga, which was a
report about the standing points of Socialist Party.
Called on German and Italian Ambassadors about the severe changes
of situation.
Went with Mr. GA to be present at the ceremony of Chilean Univer-
sity Law College giving titles to Dr. Cruchaga.
Mr. Riveros promised to give us the money.
June 18. Called on German Ambassador. He was relatively optimis-
tic, but my opinion is that the situation is very serious. Any-
how, our information was more or less the same.
Went to Chilean Bank and Central Bank at noon with Mr. Takada,
to draw out 200,000 Yen.
Mr. GA came to confirm that the information he had told me yes-
terday was quite true. He also gave me a new report.
June 19. Mr. Nabali (?) of Italian Embassy called on me, discussed
many things.
June 20.

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-5
June 20. Went to Mr. Riveros at 1l P.M., inquired about the
problem of freezing and other matters.
Mr. GA came in the afternoon, from whom I heard many interesting
things. June 2l. Mr. Fernândez came to tell me that the "balance
de Guerra" was written by Mr. Gajardo. He also asked to have
Japanese films.
June 22. I felt rather strange about the news in El Mercurio
when I was in bed last night. I got up then, wrote a cable and
sent it immediately.
Went to German Embassy this morning, talked with German Secre-
tary. I found that he seemed to have heard something already
from Melo (a Director of Foreign Office) yesterday. I was
surprised at his active ability.
Asked Mr. Nagamine to go to Foreign Office to arrange my audience
with Minister.
Interviewed Foreign Minister at 4 P.M., the result was very serious,
just as I had anticipated. Wrote a cable and sent it immediately.
The information is worse as time goes on. I must consider the
situation very grave.
To send a money draft to Japan is very difficult now, by the
kindness of Mr. Riveros, Commissioner, he allowed me only in
character of Minister to send a draft to Japan. But the situation
is very bad, I should ask him to send 10,000 yen more, as a last
chance.
June 23. Ment to Forelgn Office myself at 11 A.M., to arrange
my audience with Foreign Minister. Unfortunately the Minister
was not there. Met German Secretary at Foreign Office. He told
me that German Ambassador was at the Italian Embassy, so I went to
Italian Embassy on my way back. Talked with German and Italian
Ambassadors avout what we had talked yesterday. Also told them
that I am trying to interview Foreign Minister today. Both Am-
bassadors advised me to take a strong attittde. The Italian Am-
bassador said that Foreign Minister is an American "walking-dog",
but I thought inasmuch as the Foreign Minister was called an
oppositionist of rupture policy with Axis, we have to help him.
Interview with Foreien Minister. Told him about what I had de-
liberated and made an additional explanation of the last inter-
view. The Foreign Minister will make e speech regarding foreign
policy in the Senate today. I was greatly satisfied at having
interview beforehand.
Sent cable. Got in touch with German and Italian Ambassadors.
June 24. Went to the Senete, and to the office of President of
Senate. Talked with him hurriedly. The President of the Sen-
ate told me that there is nothing to worry about.
June 25. Called on German Ambassador, asked him about the report
of Foreign Minister to the Senate yesterday, but the Ambassador
had not yet been well informed. However some Deputies of the
Cámara came yesterday, saying that we had won. Mr. Shiberula (?)
called on me and brought me some good news. But the thing is,
it was not the present Foreign Minister, but the former one.
Moreover, it's difficult to know how far it could be effective.
Got the result in detail at 9 P.M. about the Senate deliberation.
Sent cable in Spanish.
June 26. Naval Attaché came to tell me what he had di scussed with
German Air Attaché about the matter of ships.
The war news is very good. The attitude of Chilean neutral policy
is always clear following the good news.
July 6.

=== Page 160 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-6-
July 6. Mr. Gon called on me this morning. Told Naval and
Military Attachés about what Mr. Gon had said. The situation
is very bad. President Ríos begun to uncertain and we had no
ways of restraining it.
Called on Mr. Huneeus and Italian Ambassador.
July 8. Conferred with Mr. Takada about the measure of remit-
tance.
July 9. The situation is very confused. Went to see Riveros,
Chairman of Committee of remittance, to ask him about the atti-
tude of Radical Party. He answered frankly. The newspaper in-
formed that Conference of Central Banks in Washington is deciding
to enforce the fund freezing of Axis powers. So I asked him to
prom1se us to draw out a sum of l00,c00 yen from bank and help me
to send a draft for myself to Japan. Besides, we conferred also
about the matter of ships.
July 10. Went to the Remittance Control Bureau, but they said
that according to the new instructions from government they can't
allow sending drafts to Japan.
Invited Mr. Melo to sukiyaki. I was informed that Mr. Rossetti
has changed his attitude.
July ll. Mr. Vegas called on me saying that there will be a
mass meeting at Caupolican tomorrow, organized by the neutral
policy supporters, and asked me to listen to radio.
July 14. Received an instruction of scolding from Gaimusho today.
July 15. Tried to arrange an audience with Foreign Minister but
had not got the reply.
July 16. The most important things to be preoccupied about are,
first, how to undertake the instruction of scolding, and second,
to send a draft to Japan to prepare for the bad situation.
Called on German Ambassador. I was informed about the corres-
pondence restrictions.
Asked Mr. Nagamine to hurry up the Foreign Ministry for my aud-
ience with the Foreign Minister. It is said that I have to wait
until 20th. It 1s a nuisance.
I know it is very difficult to execute this scolding instruction.
The Military Attaché said also that it is very dengerous to take
such a step. But this is an order from Gaimusho, it can't be
helped.
July 17. Went to Bureau of Remittance Control, they told me that
my application for remittence of 10,oo0 yen to Japan was already
permitted.
Pondered about the next interview with Foreign Minister. The
Naval Attache also admitted the delicate nature of this case.
July 18. Mr. E. called on me this morning. Sent cable. The
Spanish National Day. The members of Axis side were all present
at a cocktail party.
July 20. Interview with Foreign Minister at 4 P.M. Had prepared
carefully but the result was not so good. Military Attaché came
to tell me that he had conferred already with Argentine Military
Attaché about the intimate relationship between Chile and Argen-
tina.
July 27. German Ambassador called on me this afternoon. Talked
about many things.
The Mitchell's talk is changing a little,
I was told. We have a different point of view, but anyhow this
is an interesting way of looking at it.

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 3587 of February 11,
1946, from the American Embassy at Ottawa.
AIR MAIL
C C P Y
DEPARTMENT OF EXTERNAL ARFAIRS
CANADA
Ottawa, February 4, 1946
CONFT DENTIAL
Dear r. Atherton:
You will recall that you spoke to Mr. Wrong at
the end of October concerning the exmination of the
archives of the Japanese Legation and Consuls in
Canada which were to be transferred to the custody of
the Canadian government by the protecting power.
The transfer has now been completed and we are
waiting for the material to be shipped to Ottawa. We
have not yet received inventories of the archives
taken over by the Custodian's representatives in
Winnipeg, Ottawa, Toronto and Lontreal, but me anwhile
I am enclosing en inventory of the archives in Vancouver
which may be some uide to the bulk we may expect to
receive from the other offices. I shall forward copies
of the remaining inventories to you when they are received.
You will see that the material is fairly extensive
and it may take some time for us to examine it. In this
connection Mr. E. H. Norman is expected to return shortly
from Japan and will probably participate in the examina-
tion with representatives of other interested Government
departments. It has occurred to me that you might think
it desirable for interested United States of ficials to
take part in a joint examination with our officials
here. We should welcome such a solution and would be
lad to have your views on the question at your conven-
ience.
Yours sincerely,
(s) N. A. Robertson
Under Secretary of State
for External Affairs

AIR MAIL
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch
No. 13,509 of Feb. 14, 1946,
American Embassy, Santiago.
COPY
LEGACION DE SUECIA
Santiago, February 6, 1946.
Dear Mr. Ambassador,
Referring to your letter of January 26 concerning the
funds left to this Legation for expenditures in connection
with the routine protection of Japanese interests in Chile,
I beg to inform you, that, according to the estimations of
this Legation, the original sum of $ 304,544:18 (Chilean
pesos) will be sufficient until about December 31, 1946.
This Legation has come to this conclusion by calculating
with quarterly expenditures of about $ 60.000 (Chilean pesos).
Concerning the request for a report on the expenditure
of funds for the period November 6, 1945, to December 31,
1945, I am pleased enclosing such a report, which I hope
will prove sufficient. If there are any details that you
find unclear, I shall be glad to give you all explanations
required. When the time has elapsed your Embassy will be
furnished with a similar report for the period January 1
to March 31, 1946.
Finally I beg to inform you that I have taken due note
of your statement that, according to information by the
Department of State, in connection with the routine protec-
tion of Japanese interests by the Protecting Powers concerned,
there is at present no authorization for extraordinary ex-
penditures such as repatriation, which will be subject to
further study and advice, and also that the Department of
State considers that the following types of expenses are
properly allowable: 1) Relief to indigent Japanese nationals;
2) fixed expenses of the Protecting Power incidental to rou-
tine operations; and 3) charges for moving and storage of
Japanese diplomatic and consular property and archives.
Yours very sincerely,
(Sgd.) Tore Högstedt
Atrue ropy ef

Copacests
In reply, I have the honor to inform the/Department,
confirming the mbassy's despatches nos. 474/of September
28, 1945 and 553 of November 19, 1945, that the only
lwar
archives found in the spanish Legation, and delivered to
this Embassy on August 27, 1945, were the items listed in
Navy
the enclosure to my despatch no. 474, referred to above.
hB
These archives contained nothing of particular inter-

894.414/2-2746
fe
specific inquiries, they are completely devoid of any com-
munications or other correspondence and of any cryptographic
material.
e s
An investigation recently conducted by the Embassy in
fe
regard to Japanese funds has revealed, however, that there
is a blocked account in the Central Reserve Bank of El
Salvador in the name of the Legación de el Japón, amounting
3/28/46
to 9, O20 colones. As far as the mbassy can ascertain,
this sum probably represents Japanese relief money formerly
handled by the Spanish Legation and also the proceeds of
the sale of the former Japanese Legation car.
As the Foreign Office has informed me that there are
no Japanese now residing in El salvador and as consequently
there would seem to be no possible use for the funds in
question in connection with relief to inaigent Japanese
nationals, fixed expenses of protecting powers incidental
to routine operations, or charges for moving and storage,
it would seem that these funds should properly be deliv-
ered to this Embassy, acting on behalf of the four pro-
tecting powers.
The Embassy has thererore brought this matter to the
attention of the Foreign Minister in its note of February
C
19. The Embassy's understanding was pointed out in this
S
note
1VJ
APR 1 1348

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
- 2 -
note that this money would not be required for normal
expenses such as relief of individual Japanese citizens.
It was consequently requested that the Foreign Minister
give to the Embassy an expression of the opinion of the
Foreign Office as to the proper disposition which should
be made of these funds. The note also said that, should
it be convenient to have the funds in question delivered
to the Embassy, we should be glad to make appropriate dis-
position of them.
In as much as there will apparently be no current
disbursements chargeable to these funds, it is the Em-
bassy's understanding that the Department would not wish
to have from it the usual quarterly reports along the
lines indicated in the last paragraph of the Department's
circular airgram of January 8, 1946.
I shall report further developments in this matter
as they may occur.
Respectfully yours,

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.97)
CONFIDENTIAL
To the
Officer in Charge of the American Mission,
894.414/2-2746
San Salvador.
The Secretary of State refers to the Embassy's des-
patch of February 27, 1945 concerning Japanese records,
archives and property and advising that Japanese funds to
the amount of 9.O20 colones are blocked in the Central Re-
serve Bank of El Salvador.
The Department is of the opinion that in as much as
the amount is of no particular significance as far as repa-
rations are concerned and in view of the position taken by
the Department in its circular instruction of November 11,
1942, and circular airgram of February 2, 1943 that if the
Salvadoran Government is not willing to transfer these funds
to the custody of the representatives of the Four Allied
Powers it may not be desirable to press the Salvadoran Gov-
ernment for such transfer.
The Department assumes that the Salvadoran Government
will take no action as to the final disposition of these
funds until the whole question of the ultimate disposition
of Japanese external assets is decided.

four Ambassadors who are to take over the Japanese funds,

Swedish Minister has intimated that the one filing case of
archives which is stored in his Legation, he believes con-
tains correspondence between the Japanese and Germans
which might be of interest to us. Therefore we think that
we should insist upon having these archives brought over to
the premises of our Embassy and not yield to the Russian
request that they be sent to the Russian Embassy.
The new instructions which the Swedish Minister has
just received are to the effect that he is to retain a
sufficient amount of the funds on hand to enable him to
continue to pay sums to Japanese nationals who, the Swedish
Minister claims, are not allowed to work in Mexico. This
C

S

6

instigation of General MacArthur, whereby the funds, archives
and property (excluding private possessions) were to be
turned over to the four Ambassadors.

LIAISON
SUBJECT: Disposal bf Japanese Archives, Funds and Proderty
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
ta-5-18B-t
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to despatch No. 27,994 of
January 19, 1946, with regard to the disposal of Japanese
archives, funds and property; to the Department's circular
airgram of January 23, 1946, on the same subject; to the
894.414/3-246
Department's secret telegram No. 94 of January 30, 1946,
and to the Department's circular airgram dated January 31,
12:30 p.m., 1946, with regard to the same subject.
Pursuant to the Department's several instructions in
the premises, the Department is informed that a meeting
was convened at the Embassy on Wednesday, February 27,
1946, at which were present the Ambassadors of the United
States, Great Britain, Russia and China, and a memorandum
of the conversations fully reporting the meeting is en-
closed herewith.
With respect to the property it was agreed that it is
to be sold and the proceeds are to be put in a joint bank
account, and that private property belonging to Japanese
nationals is to be left in the care of the Swedish Minister,
who is the representative or the protecting power.
It was also agreed that funds belonging to the Japa-
nese Government would be taken over by the four Ambassadors
and deposited in the Bank of Mexico in a joint account.
CE/VJ
It was agreed with respect to the archives that they
would be taken over by the representatives of the four
powers, and that the single filing case which might con-
tain documents of interest would be examined before
deciding where these documents would be kept, the American,
Chinese and British Ambassadors being in favor of storing
these archives in the American Embassy.
A sub-committee/
MAR 1-11946
 L A YOREAN
41473.246
ECONOMIC AS
DEPARTMENT OF STATE4
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
MAY 31946

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.97)
AIR MAIL
- 2 -
A sub-committue was appointed for making arrangements
to carry out the details of the transfer, and further re-
ports will be rena red to the Department as the matter
progresses. The decision to effect the delivery on Tues-
day, March 5, will have to be postponed in view of new
instructions received by the Swedish Minister with regard
to the delivery of the finances. It will be necessary in
view of these instructions for the Swedish Minister to
confer with the four Ambassadors before the formal act of
delivery can be made; and a further report with reference
to this phase of the matter will be promptly transmitted
to the Department.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador,

=== Page 205 === (confidence 1.00)
AIR MAIL
(Enclosure to Despatch No. 28,703 of March 2, 1946)
MEMORANDUM
of
Meeting Held in the Office of the American
Ambassador on Wednesday, February 27, 1946,
at Which were Present, Ambassador George S.
Messersmith, Chairman; Ambassador C. T. Feng,
(China); Ambassador Charles H. Bateman,
(Great Britain); Ambassador Alexander N.
Kapustin, (Russia), and Mr. Vasili P. Yaku-
bovsky, First Secretary of the Russian
Embassy, and Mr. Raymond H. Gesit, Counselor
of the American Embassy.
Subject: Disposition of Japanese Property,
Funds and Archives.
The American Ambassador opened up the meeting which
was convoked to discuss the question of Japanese property,
funds and archives by stating that he thought it would
be advisable to discuss the question of Japanese archives
taking up the property first, the funds second, and the
archives last.
PROPERTY
The American Ambassador observed that it was clear
there was no real estate owned by the Japanese and that
the premises were rented; and that so far as property is
concerned, it appears from the inventory that there is
only furniture. The Ambassador stated that the United
States Government is disposed to take either of two
courses with regard to Japanese property: (1) to sell
the property and deposit the funds, or (2) to store it.
The latter course he thought not desirable, as it would
meen spending money uselessly. He thought that an agree-
ment could be reached among the four representatives as
to which course to take. He observed, however, that his
Government had raised the question of private property
(which was not to be sold or disposed of according to
his instructions) because in some countries private pro-
perty had been left behind. The American Ambassador re-
called the case of the Czechoslovakian Legation, of
which the property and archives were returned the other
day to the new Czech Minister, to which function the
Ambassador was invited as Dean of the Diplomatic Corps.
The Czech Minister noted that certain articles which
originally were turned over to the Germans were missing,
while at the same time he noticed packing cases marked
as belonging to private individuals of the former
German Legation.
The Ambassador/

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
- 2 -
The Ambassador observed, with regard to the Japa-
nese funds, that according to his instructions the
United States Government prefers that funds taken over
from the Swedes be deposited in an account in the name
of the four Chiefs of Mission, which funds could only
be withdrawn by checks signed by the four Chiefs of
Mission. The Ambassador suggested in this connection
that the funds be deposited not in the individual names
of the Ambassadors, but in the name of the American
Ambassador, the British Ambassador, etc.
The British Ambassador inquired if this would need
a trustee. The American Ambassador said he thought not,
but that point might be explored.
With regard to the payments that were being made to
the Japanese Committee on behalf of Japanese nationals,
the Ambassador stated that his Government was in favor
of going into this question tc see if these payments
should be continued. The British Ambassador pointed out
that a lot of money was being paid out; therefore, he
thought the lease should be cancelled at once. The
American Ambassador said that he thought so tog but he
considered it might be wise to exercise some caution in
this respect, as he found that the Mexican Government
had been keeping the German Legation because there was no
place to store the effects; and before the lease could be
cancelled an arrangement would have to be made to sell
the articles now stored in the Japanese premises. The
British Ambassador inquired about the expediency of
continuing the employment of caretakers, gardeners, etc.
At this point the American Ambassador took up the
question of archives, and stated that in his opinion
they could either be held here for examination or sent
to Washington.
The Russian Ambassador interposed by saying that
the Russian Embassy desires to inspect the archives here.
The American Ambassador said that we have nobody in
the American Embassy knowing Japanese, but pointed out
that in his instructions it was suggested that a group
of experts could come from the several Embassies to
examine the archives.
The discussion was resumed with regard to funds;
and the American Ambassador repeated that the funds
should be deposited and withdrawn only by an agreement
in each case by the four Ambassadors. The British
Ambassador said that the only question which arises is
what funds are to be paid out. At this point in the
discussion the American Ambassador thought it would be
advisable to get some decision with regard to the dis-
position of property. The Russian Ambassador said he
was/

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-3-
was in agreement that all official property should be sold
and the money divided. The British Ambassador inquired
of the Russian Ambassador if he meant by this that a
distinction should be made between the funds taken over
which should be deposited and the proceeds of the sale
which he understood the Russian Ambassador suggested be
divided.
The American Ambassador said the proceeds of the
sale should, of course, be put in the same fund, with
which the Russian Ambassador agreed saying "that was all
right". The American Ambassador wanted to know if all
agreed that the property should be sold, to which the
other three Ambassadors gave their assent, and it was
agreed to take all the property over and sell it by
auction. The Russian Ambassador observed that private
property must be excluded, to which the other Ambassadors
agreed.
As the conversation had reverted again to private
property, the American Ambassador spoke confidentially,
saying that when the Czech Minister took over the effects
he declared that he wanted to make an observation to the
Mexican Foreign Office tc the effect that he did not see
linens, pictures, books, crystals and other objects that
were in the Czech Legation when the Germans took it over,
and that before he signed a document indicating the re-
turn of the Czech property he wished to put his observa-
tions in an official note. The American Ambassador said
that as he was there as a witness and before he signed as
a witness, he wished to say that in his opinion the
Czech Minister was justified in making the Foreign Office
officially cognizant of the disappeerance of certain
articles. The American Ambassador said he noticed the
packing cases which were marked "personal property",
some of which packing cases he thought might contain
property formerly belonging to the Czech Legation.
Coming to the point of the discussion again, the
American Ambassador observed that apparently the unani-
mous agreement of the Ambassadors is as follows:
Property is to be sold and the proceeds are
to be put in a joint bank account;
2。
Private property is to be kept.
At this point the American Ambassador desired to
make the following statement:
If private property is not identifiable it
is to be sold.
FUNDS

=== Page 208 === (confidence 1.00)
AIR MAIL
-4-
FUNDS
The American Ambassador said that they would now take
up the question of the Japanese funds, and inquired what
bank the other Ambassadors would suggest. The British
Ambassador suggested the Bank of Mexico if it takes accounts.
The American Ambassador said that that would be the best
bank, and inquired what is a second choice, observing that
it would be advisable to keep the funds in a Mexican bank.
No one suggested a second choice.
With regard to payments, the American Ambassador
asked if these had gone beyond caretakers, gardeners, etc.
The British Ambassador said that payments had been made
also to the Japanese Committee apparently for needy Japa-
nese nationals and that of course payments had been made
for the rent of the premises, telephone, electric light
and other incidentals, which of course would all stop
when the property was disposed of and the lease terminated.
The Chinese Ambassador said that he would like to inquire
with regard to the payments made to the Japanese Committee,
to which the American Ambassador replied that we should
know how these funds are used. He thought that if by dis-
continuing payments to destitute Japanese they would become
a burden on the Mexican Government, that would have to be
taken into consideration; but that in order to go into
these matters it would be necessary for each one of the
Ambassadors to designate a person to handle the details,
as the Chiefs of Mission would be unable to go into these
matters, and suggested that the Counselors of the several
Embassies go into the question as to how the money is
spent. In tnat way a quick decision in the premises could
be reached.
ARCHIVES
The American Ambassador observed that he understood
that according to instructions received by the Soviet
Ambassador his Government desired that he examine the
archives, which could be examined at the American Embassy
or they could be moved to some other place, the archives
not being voluminous. The American Ambassador said the
archives could be stored in a room in the American Embassy
where they would be available for examination. The
Chinese Ambassador said he had a secretary who could read
Japanese, that is Mr. Chang. The Russian Ambassador said
that his Mr. Grigori Kasparov could read Japanese. The
American Ambassador suggested that after the archives have
been examined we could best determine what to do with
them, all the Ambassadors proceeding in this way on a
common basis. The British Ambassador thought that to get
these matters handled quickly it would be best to name
the persons who would constitute a sub-committee to look
after/

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)

after all the details. The Ambassadors then named their
representatives as follows:
Mr. Raymond H. Geist, Counselor, for the
American Embassy;
Mr. Edward O. Coote, First Secretary, for
the British Embassy;
Mr. Tien-Yuen Chang, First Secretary, for
the Chinese Embassy;
Mr. Vasili P. Yakubovsky, Counselor, for
the Russian Embassy.
It was agreed that these officers should first look into
the amounts paid to the Japanese Committee, and then
arrange for the sale within a month of the effects, and
then give notice of the lease, etc.
The American Ambassador then observed that it would
be necessary to execute a document which the four Ambassa-
dors could sign, and that it could be arranged to take
over the property within the next few days. The Ambassador
explained in general the form of the document and how it
should be drawn up.
At this juncture the British Ambassador called atten-
tion to the fact that certain checks listed in the invent-
ory were dated 1941 and 1942 and that obviously those
checks would be out of date. The American Ambassador said
that the Swedish Minister, of course, would turn over the
checks as he has them in his possession, and that naturally
we could not expect him to turn over funds instead of
checks; but that these matters could be gone into by the
four gentlemen appointed to look into all these questions.
The American Ambassador then inquired as to whether it
would be advisable to have a meeting with the Swedish
Minister, and the British Ambassador thought it would.
The American Ambassador suggested that it might be advis-
able beforehand to prepare a formal document of what the
Swedish Minister is turning over to the four Ambassadors.
The American Ambassador then called a stenographer and
dictated the following suggested form:
"The Minister of Sweden in Mexico, Mr.
Herbert de Ribbing, acting on behalf of the
Japanese Government and in possession of the
archives, property, and funds of the former
Japanese Legation in Mexico since the receipt
thereof from the Protuguese Legation on July
9, l945, is delivering, under instructions of
the Japanese Government received through his

funds to the principal diplomatic representa-

"The/

After this was completed the American Ambassador
observed that the first meeting with the Swedish Minister
should be with the four Ambassadors; and then inquired
where we should deposit the archives. He suggested they
be examined in the British Embassy. The Chinese and the
British Ambassadors suggested that the archives should be
brought to the American Embassy where they could all
examine them. The American Ambassador said this was
satisfactory; as there is room in the American Embassy
where these archives can be stored. The Russian Ambassa-
dor observed that the examination of the archives would
not take long; and then the British Ambassador read a list
of the files as they are contained in the inventory, and
concluded his remarks by saying, "The sooner the property
is sold the better".
The American Ambassador stated that he assumed the
files contained really nothing of interest - probably
only formal correspondence and records of Japanese citizens,
and that all other matters had been meticulously destroyed.
There was no real concern as to where the files are
actually kept; but they should be in one of the four
missions. It might be more appropriate under the circum-
stances for them to be kept here in the American Embassy.
There is a room available on the second floor where the
documents would be easily accessible to the representatives
of the other Embassies. Both the Chinese and the British
Ambassador again repeated that they thought it would be
advisable to store the archives in the American Embassy,
whereupon the Russian Ambassador said that he would like
to/

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-7-
to have them in his Embassy. The American Ambassador said
there was no objection in principle; as, as a matter of
general procedure, the American Embassy is an appropriate
place to have them. It may be that some of the documents
are in Spanish as well as in other languages.
The question then arose as to how long it would take
to examine the documents; and the British Ambassador
thought that Mr. Chang and Mr. Kasparov could do it
immediately - probably within a week - and then it could
be determined if they were of any value. The American
Ambassador said again that this could best be done here
in the American Embassy, and that he would have no objec-
tion to giving Mr. Kasparov and Mr. Chang the keys to the
room in which the archives are stored; as, in fact, all
that we wish to know is what is in them.
The discussion then reverted to the question of the
document, which in the meantime had been typed by the
stenographer and had been brought in and distributed to
the several Ambassadors. After reading the document the
Americen Ambassador observed that the final form of this
would have to be discussed with Mr. de Ribbing, and that
the Counselor, Mr. Geist, could call on him tomorrow and
get an agreement. The Russian Ambassador then again
brought up the question of the archives and suggested that
they be examined in the Swedish Legation by Mr. Kasparov
and Mr. Chang. The American Ambassador resumed the con-
versation with regard to the visit Mr. Geist was to make
to the Swedish Minister. He was to tell him about the
meeting and ask if the form were satisfactory; and then
the Swedish Minister could prepare five copies of the
document with five copies of the inventory. The American
Ambassador suggested this might be done Friday or Saturday.
At the same time it ∞ uld be determined if the Bank of
Mexico could take the account and be agreeable to having
withdrawals made on the signature of the four Chiefs of
Mission. When that is arranged the Swedish Minister will
transfer the account. After that the sub-committee
appointed could make arrangements to sell the effects;
and immediately after the transfer is made the sub-committee
could arrange to have the documents examined by Mr. Kasparov
and Mr. Chang.
Then the American Ambassador mentioned code books,
and said that if any existed his Government would like to
have copies without delay, of which photostats could be
made. The other Ambassadors assented to this.
The American Ambassador suggested that on Tuesday
afternoon at 4 p.m., March 5, 1946, the Ambassadors could
meet with the Swedish Minister at the Swedish Legation and
formally take over the Japanese property, effect the
document, etc.
At this point the American Ambassador telephoned to
Mr. de/

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
- 8 -
Mr. de Ribbing, the Swedish Minister. After the conversa-
tion the American Ambassador said that the Swedish Minister
agreed to having the transfer effected on Tuesday, Maroh 5,
at 4 p.m. In the telephone conversation the Swedish
Minister, in reply to the inquiries of the American Ambassa-
dor, had stated that the archives were in the premises of
the former Japanese Legation, except one case of files
which could be transferred back to the Japanese Legation.
The British Ambassador inquired if the others thought
it expedient that a Japanese citizen continue to be the
guard at the former Japanese Legation premises after the
transfer had been made. To this the American Ambassador
suggested that we should employ somebody else to be in
charge. The British Ambassador reminded that the property
in the former Japanese Legation may be of value and that
somebody must be in charge there that would prevent theft;
but the American Ambassador pointed out that it might not
be expedient to get rid of the personnel from one day to
the other, and that undouotedly a policeman could be
obtained from the Mexican Government. The Chinese Ambassa-
dor suggested two policemen.
In summing up, the American Ambassador observed that
we should proceed as rapidly as possible with the sale of
the property and the examination of the archives, and that
the committee appointed should get together at once to go
into the question of (1) payment of funds; (2) personnel
employed; (3) termination of the lease, telephone, etc.;
and (4) sale of the property.

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
SPD
TEXT OF FOREIGN OFFICE CIRCULAR TO CERTAIN
BRITISH DIPLOMATIC MISSIONS, DATED FEBRUARY
23rd, 1946.
Addressed to His Majesty's Representatives at the
Holy See, Angora, Beirut, Brussels, Sofia, Baghdad, Copenhagen,
Rome, Macao, Oslo, Tehran, Lisbon, Resident Minister's Office,
Central Mediterranean, Caserta, Bucharest, Madrid, Stockholm,
and Berne.
subject to the concurrence of your United States, Soviet
and Chinese colleagues,where applicable, the following are my views
concerning the disposal of Japanese diplomatic and consular
archives and property in the country to which you are accredited.
They are not intended to apply in cases where it has been agreed
by the Four Powers, or may subsequently be agreed by them, that
the Government in whose territory Japanese archives and property
are located may itself take sole custody. In such cases my view
is tiat assumption of custody imposes upon the Government in
question the responsibility for the disposal of property (and
ultimately of the archives also) subject only to the right of the
Four Powers to obtain access, within a period to be agreed between
the Government of that country and the representatives of the
Four Powers, t) the archives if they wish to do so.
Archives.
2.
For your information I consider it unlikely that the
archives in the country to which you are accredited will contain
any material of value, and unless specifically advised by you of
the presence of documents which, as far as you are able to judge,
may contain such material, I do not propose to make special
arrangements for the examination of archives. Unless you see
reason for desiring an examination, I would therefore suggest that
you propose to your colleagues that after a suitable lapse of
time to enable those who so desire to examine them, the archives
shoulu be destroyed or otherwise disposed of by agreement among
your colleagues and yourself. should any of your colleagues
desire, after the interval mentioned above, to arrange for the
transport or the arcnives to his country for further examination
I would see no objection.
3。
soe posts to which this telegram is addressed have
already reported that the archives have been examined and found
to contain nothing of interest. In such cases, only action
required is to arrange for disposal.
Immovable official property
4.
Where diploma ic or consular buildings or offices are
rented, the lease should be terminated as soon as practicable.
Where buildings are the property of the Japanese Government they
should be sold or put up for lease depending on local conditions.
The proceeds should be paid into an account to be administered
pending its disposal by yourself and your three colleagues
where applicable.
Other official property.
5.
All official furniture and other movable official
property should be sold, except the following: archives, stamps,
seals, Japanese or Axis flags or other insignia, pictures, books
or pamphlets of pro-Japanese or pro-Axis propagandist nature.
The proceeds should be paid to the account referred to in paragraph
4 above.

=== Page 221 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
6.
Stamps and seals, if any, should be held in joint
custody pending agreement among the Four Powers as to their
disposal. I would see no objection to their destruction under
supervision of a representative of each of the Four Powers where
present but ycu should not agree to the representative of a
single Power assuming sole custody of them.
7.
I am in principle opposed to the sale of flags, insignia,
propagandist books or pamphlets or any other article of sentimental
or ideological interest to possible pro-apanese or pro-Axis
sympathisers. such articles should in my view be destroyed. I
would however see no objection to the sale of books not falling
under this category.
8.
All expenses incurred in connexion with the custody of
archives anu the sale of property should be borne equally by your
three colleagues, where applicable, and yourself, and should
eventually be recovered from the proceeds of the sale of official
property or from other Japanese funds held by you. Your share in
these transactions should appear in your quarterly account as a
separate item.
Frivate Froperty.
9.
1.o decision has yet been taken regarding the disposal
of private belongings of former members of Japanese diplomatic
and consular missions abroad. As in most cases the Japanese in
question have now returned to Japan, where they are under the
jurisdiction of SCAP, it must necessarily be the ultimate
responsibility of the United States authorities to make arrangements
for its disposal. In practice, I do not consider that it will be
possible to transport articles of heavy furniture, etc., to Japan
from the country to which you are accredited, and His majesty's
Government could certainly not unuertake to provide shipping space
or other facilities. In view of transport and other difficulties
it may be considered best to sell private belongings but the
Instrument of Surrender signed by Japan does not in my view
entitle the Four Powers to proceed to any final disposal of the
private belongings of Japanese inuividuals. Should the question
be raised you may speak in the above sense. If any of your
colleagues should propose the sale of private effects, you should
say that you could agree only provided your United States colleague
is satisfied that this is in accordance witn the wishes of the
owners. If he is unable to give this assurance you should with-
hold your consent pending reference of tne matter to me.
10.
The foregoing paragraph applies only to bona fide private
effects such as furniture, works of art, motorcars, etc. It does
not include liquid assets, bonds, shares or any form of currency
deposited in banks or elsewhere in the name of any private Japanese
individual. Ihese will be subject of a separate agreement among
the Powers concerned.
11。
The instructions contained in paragraphs 1 to 10 above
refer to Japanese archives and property only. As none of the Four
Powers has ever recognised "Manchoukuo" as an independent State
the attitude of the Chinese Government towards Manchoukuo
diplomatic and consular missions abroad may be that their liquidation
is a Chinese internal matter. Shoulu this view be put forward by
your Chinese colleague I would see no reason to dissent. In cases
where no Chinese representative is present you should take no
further action regarding "Manchoukuo" official property or archives
without prior reference to me.

Plans for disposal Japanese Legation property agreed
upon today at conference with Soviet and British officials.
(Dept's 22, Feb 8 and ciroular airgram Jan 31). Soviets
have instructions from Moscow stating simply it favors
dividing property among the four nations. British have
no instructions but agree that:
(1) Safes will be broken and archives and codes
sealed by three legations and stored at US Legation.
(2) Private property of Japanese officials will be
stored at commercial warehouse, storage costs to be charged
against goods.
(3) All govt property will be inventoried and valued.
In view of lack of goods in Pinland and slight value of
Finnish marks British official and I concur with Soviet
suggestion that we consult on allowing each Legation to
select items of official property for its own use end
debit appraised value against that nations share. Balance
will be sold as storage considered impractical.
(4) Work will start March 18 and effort made to
clear Legatiou before lease expires end March.

from the Embasxy at Habana
SECRET
Habana, March 18, 1946
MEMORANDUM
Transmitting photographs of regis-
tration cards from Japanese Legation Archives
Reference is made to a memorandum, enclosed with
the Embassy's despatch No. 925 of January 18, 1946,
reporting on the Japanese Legation archives and pro-
perty in Habana.
On page 2 of the memorandum in question, certain
:gistration cards of some possible interest were
oferred to by number, and the names of the persons
) whom they apply were given. In three cases brief
comment, on the basis of the work of the Cuban Japan-
ese-language readers, were made, and nine other names
were listed as being those of people who had apparently
been deliberately recallod to Japan by the Japanese
Government. The cards Bore the following numbers:
722, 729, 657, 727, 728,791, 735, 46, 72, 502, 222,
and 76.
In view of the possibility that the work of the
Japanese-language readers employed by the Cuban Minis-
try of State has not been as accurate or as complete
as the possible importance of these cards may have
called for, photographs of the cards in question have
been taken and a set of these pho tographs, bearing
Arabic numbers and names in Roman script, is enclosed
herewith. Sets of these photographs have also been
made available directly to the Military, Naval and
Legal Attachés assigned to this Embassy for trans-
mission to their respective departments.
H。B。W.
HBW/hmp

M 104
LEGATION OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Stockholm, March 18, 1946.
No. 6784
CONFIDENTIAL
Subject:
Examination of Archives of Former Japanese Legation
in Stockholm. For the Attention of Mr. Frederick B.
Lyon, Director, Office of Controls, also War Depart-
ment, Navy Department, and Commander Rufus L. Taylor.
NO.2
3 to war
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
With reference to the Department's confidential circular
894.414/3-1846
airgram No. 3610, January 31, 1946 I have the honor to report that
the archives of the former Japanese Legation in Stockholm are kept
in locked safes in the premises of that former Legation, which now
are occupied by the American Legation, and that they are available
during business hours to representatives of the Inter-Allied Comaittee
for Control of Japanese Government Property in Sweden.
An examination of these archives was conducted during January 7
to January 11, 1946 by Commander Rufus L. Taylor, U. S. N. A copy
is enclosed of Commander Taylor's memorandum dated January 14, 1946
concerning the examination of the archives (enclosure No. 1). Selec-
ted passages of this memorandum were submitted to the British, Chinese
and Russian members of the Inter-Allied Comnittee. It appears from
the memorandum that Commander Taylor found no documents of real and
important intelligence value in the Japanese archives here.
The docunents of possible interest listed in enclosure No. 1
are transmitted under separate cover as enclosure No. 2 to this
despatch. As indicated by Commander Taylor, these documents are
duplicate copies. They have been selected by Commander Taylor and
removed from the arehives with the consent of the Inter-Allied
Committee.
According to information obtained from a member of the former
Japanese Legation, who has been repatriated, the code machine of the
former Japanese Legation was broken up prior to the formal surrender
to the Inter-Allied Comaittee of Japanese Government Property in
Sweden, and the parts were thrown into a canal, whence they were
recovered by the Swedish police. Swedish authorities have been
notified of this report, but no confirmation thereof has been
received

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch No. 6784, dated March 18, 1946 from
the American Legation, Stockholm, entitled: "Examination of
Archives of Former Japanese Legation in Stockholm. For the
Attention of r. Frederick B. Lyon, Director, Office of Controls
also War Department, Navy Department, and Commander Rufus L. Taylor."
COPY
CONFIDENTIAL
14 January 1946
MEMO TO:
Naval Attache, American Legation, Stockholm.
Subject:
Archives of former Japanese Legation in Stockholm,
Examination of.
Encl.:
(A) Log of Activities.
(B) List of material of possible intelligence value.
(C) Copy of Liemo, dated 8 January 1946.
1.
I have completed examination of archives of the former
Japanese Legation in Stockhoim and have found nothing of value
except as noted in Enclosure (B).
2.
The Japanese had adequate time to do a thorough job of
disposing of material of possible intelligence value and apparently
did so.
3.
In the course of this examination several points of
interest were noted:
(a)
Personnel of the former Japanese Legation and personnel
of the former Japanese Naval Mission in Berlin are in Sweden
under technical arrest in the case of the former and under
internment in the case of the latter.
(b)
Included amongst these personnel are Major General ONODERA,
the former Military Attache in Stockholm and probable chief of a
broad intelligence organization in Northern Europe, and Vice
Admiral ABE, who was head of the Japanese Naval Missi on in Germany.
Included with ABE are some 20 of his assistants.
(c)
Strategic Service has a rather comprehensive file on all
Japanese operating in Scandinavia including dossiers on the above
personnel. This file is already of interest from a counter-
intelligence point of view and would be of value in aiding the
extraction of direct intelligence through interrogation.
(d)
Uur Legation has obtained considerable economic intelli-
gence of interest through interrogation of Japanese press personnel
and personnel of the former Jap Legation, and through their own
examination of subject archives. Some of this information bears
on the economic situation of Sweden.
(e) There
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
Stockholm, Despatch No. 6784
Enclosure No. 1
-2-
(e) There would be no objection on the part of Sweden to the
interrogation of such personnel as might be considered appropriate.
4.
It is recommended:
(a)
That no further exploitation of subject archives be under-
taken by the Navy;
(b) That steps be taken to extract by interrogation information
from Japanese personnel now in Sweden but that such interrogation
be carefully prepared both here and in Washington as suggested
in Enclosure (c);
(c) That if and when such interrogation takes place steps be
taken to minimize the likelihood of our line of interrogation
or any valuable information extracted therefrom becoming known
to the other interested powers;
(d)
) That in case of imminent departure from Sweden of the
above Japanese nationals no action should be taken to detain
them unless an affirmative answer has been received to the
Legation's telegram sent in accordance with your recommendations
based on the suggestions contained in Enclosure (C).
(e)
That no interrogations be conducted here without direct
instructions from Washington covering the scope and security of
such action even if such a course means that these Japanese can-
not then be interrogated until they reach Japan.
5.
Inasmuch as we are committed to joint exploitation of sub-
ject archives and information related to Japanese connected therewith,
and some of the statements in this memo as well as certain remarks in
Enclosure (A) might operate to our disadvantage if known to the other
interested powers, it is considered that this report and documents
related directly thereto should be classified as confidential matter.
Respectfully,
Rufus L. Taylor
Commander, USN.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 237 === (confidence 0.99)
Stockholm, Despatch No. 6784
Enclosure No. 1
-3-
CONFIDENTIAL
LOG OF ACTIVITIES
3 Jan 1946 - Commenced photographing documents, completed reports and
made arrangements to proceed to Stockholm.
4 Jan 1946 - 1030. Took off for Stockholm in Naval Attache's plane,
stopped at Bremen for gas and proceeded on to Copenhagen,
arriving at 1515. Unable to proceed further today due to bad weather
and darkness.
5 Jan 1946 - Weathered in at Copenhagen.
6 Jan 1946 - Same.
7 Jan 1946 - Took off for Stockholm at 1030 and arrived at 1215. Met
and treated with great courtesy. Held conference re Jap
archives with ir. Knox and ir. Lium of our Legation and went to Jap
Legation for preliminary inspection. Everything most efficiently
arranged for examination. Files, safes and personal baggage open, every-
thing numbered, a man in attendance throughout the day. The arrange-
ment for exploitation is that four-power committee has custody and each
member has free entry with the understanding that nothing is to be
removed and substance of any information gleaned is to be reported to
the committee. Mr. Knox is chairman. No decision has yet been made
re final disposition of archives.
8 Jan 1946 - Reported personally to Naval Attache, Capt. Wright. Was
called in conference with Messrs. Knox, Lium and Ravndal,
our Charge d'Affaires, and decided that our most likely source of
information is the Japs who are still here. These include Admiral Abe
and General Onodera. Such action, we agreed, should be carefully
planned and not hurried. Wrote memo re this to Attache. Then proceeded
to Chancery of Jap Legation at 57 Strandvagen. Examined files and
personal luggage in four rooms including that of Military Attache and
the code room. Nothing of real value observed, except possibly a small
file of traffic in 5-letter cipher. Do not believe archives will be
productive as thorough cleaning out job has been done. Again, as in
Paris, ahredding machines and fireplaces effectively used. No worth-
while shreds or ashes remaining. Will recommend I finish examination
of files, make personal report to Washington and return (or send
someone else) to exploit interrogation as a joint Army-Navy-State effort
in conjunction with similar effort on the part of our Legation here.
Believe Legation, Military and Naval Attaches will concur. Conferred
again with Captain Wright and then with General Kessler, the Military
Attache. All agree to above recommendation. Accordingly, Lir. Knox
drafted a telegram in the name of the Legation recommending carefully
planned and briefed interrogation.
9 Jan 1946 - Spent
ENCLOSURE (A)

=== Page 238 === (confidence 0.99)
Stockholm, Despatch No. 6784
Enclosure No. 1
-4-
CONFIDENTIAL
9 Jan 1946 - Spent morning examining remaining rooms but found
nothing further except a set of confidential Jap
Foreign Office instruction and regulation books. Will arrange to
get these. Observed a current control box bearing a name plate
indicating it for use with type-9l model 3 cipher machine (same
as in Paris). Stayed at Jap Legation until 1600 when called in
conference with Messrs. Carlson and Higdon of OSS and Mr. Lium of the
Legation. Learned (a) that certain features of draft of telegram
not quite satisfactory; (b) that Strategic Services here have con-
siderable worthwhile information gathered over a long period on
Japanese activities in Scandinavia, including counter-intelligence
that would be of considerable value in exploiting interrogations
for direct intelligence.
10 Jan 1946 - Conferred with Capt. Wright and Commander Klaveness.
Called on Lium and polished draft of despatch to be
sent by State. All concerned agreed and the telegram will be sent.
Spent remainder of morning reading Legation and Strategic Service's
file on Jap activities. Find it very interesting and very likely
to be quite helpful in attempting to obtain high grade information
by means of interrogation. Proceeded to Legation and finished
examination of documents and files.
11 Jan 1946 - Commenced drawing up final report and arranging for
photographing. Spent afternoon browsing through
previously examined files just to recheck.
12 Jan 1946 - Received word that Jap nationals in Sweden may be able
to leave via PLUS ULTRA from Naples on 29 January, in which case
it will be impossible to conduct interrogation here. Conferred
with Captain Wright and interested parties in the Legation and
it was agreed to inform Washington by urgent cable of the situation
and take no action unless Washington approves suggested program, in
which case Japs will be detained. In either case it will not affect
my mission.
