CONFIDENTIAL
The below-listed items found in the Archives of the former
Japanese Legation in Stocknolm are considered of possible interest
though of low intelligence value at best. In the case of the items
1 through 5 inclusive duplicate copies of each are available and if
the Inter-Allied Control Committee can arrange for us to have the
originals they might be of use. However, I do not consider them worth
the expense of photographing.
Photographs of the other items listed will be taken and
turned over to you by Mr. Lium of the Legation.
Vr. Lium will arrange disposition of items 1 through 5
inclusive.
List of Documents of Possible Interest
1. Manual covering entrance, residence and exit of foreigners
(in 1 volume classified as confidential and promulgated by the Amer-
ican Bureau of the Japanese Ministry of Foreign Affairs in June 1937).
2. Manual covering travel and migration (in 2 volumes promulgated
by same authority and on same date as above).
3. Manual for guidance of official missions abroad (in 3 volumes
classified as secret and promulgated by Ministry of Foreign Affairs
in January 1938).
4. Same as 2 above except promulgated by Commercial Bureau of
Foreign Ministry in 1 volune on 1 January 1927.
5. Internal Regulations for the Ministry of Foreign Affairs
(in 1 volume classified as confidential and compiled in March 1923
but corrected to January 1938).
6. File of notes on economic conditions in Sweden covering
period 1939 to 1941.
7. File of 4 despatches in 5-letter cipher from Dublin to
"Minister Stockholm" via commercial communications and dated 31
October 1945.
NCLOSURE (B)

=== Page 240 === (confidence 0.99)
Stockholm, Despatch No. 6784
Enclosure No. 1
-6-
CONFIDENTIAL
8 January 1946
MFMO TO: Naval Attache, American Legation, Stockholm.
Subj.:
Archives of former Japanese Legation, exploitation of.
1.
As a result of preliminary investigation and conference with
Mr. Knox and Mr. Lium concerning the archives of the former Japanese
Legation in Stockholm the following facts have been brought out:
(a) We are committed to joint exploitation of subject
archives.
(b) A joint committee composed of a Russian, Chinese,
British and American representative has been formed with Mr. Knox
as chairman and custody of the archives has been turned over to this
Committee each member of which has free and separate access to the
archives but is bound by agreement to remove nothing. However, each
one may photograph what he pleases and is obliged to inform the other
members of the nature of such documents or objects as the case may be.
(c) The personnel of the Jap Legation are still in Sweden
under technical arrest and in little likelihood of leaving for several
months.
(d) It is possible and perhaps probable that at least some
government property of the Legation is in the private possession of the
personnel of the Legation and might be made available.
(e) Many Japanese formerly in Germany, one of whom is
Admiral Abe, are in Sweden in the same status as the former Jap Swedish
Legation personnel.
(f) One of the Japanese of the Legation still in Sweden is
Major General Onadera, former Jap Military Attache.
(g) The archives themselves can be properly examined in
three or four days and at first glance (considering circumstances) do
not seem likely to be very productive of valuable military, naval or
technical intelligence.
2。
Inasmuch as Admiral Abe and General Onadera were perhaps the
two most important Japanese military officers in Europe and as a
thorough search of personal possessions of Japanese diplomats here
might be productive, though a long and tedious process, it is recom-
mended that Washington be advised that:
(a) A broad
ENCLOSURE (C)

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.99)
Stockholm, Despatch No. 6784
Enclosure No. 1
-7-
CONFIDENTIAL
(a) A broad range program for interrogation of important
Japanese be prepared in Washington jointly by State, War and
Navy as soon as possible.
(b) That meanwhile a similar program be prepared by the
American Legation in Stockholm.
(c) That I examine archives presently available here and
after completing similar examinations in Madrid return to Wash-
ington as quickly as possible to report in person with the idea
of either returning to Stockholm prior to 1 March or if not me,
qualified personnel as deemed appropriate.
(d) That if I be returned to Stockholm it be with the
idea of remaining on in permanent status in such capacity as
may be deemed appropriate.
3。
It is considered that hasty action in conducting these
investigations might result in the loss of valuable information
whereas careful and thorough exploitation of the opportunities presented
here in Sweden might result in obtaining valuable intelligence of a
far reaching nature. Mr. Knox and Mr. Lium concur in this view and
conversation with the Charge d'Affairs, r. Ravndal, leads me to
believe that the American Legation here would concur in making the
above recommendations in so far as exploitation of these opportunities
is concerned.
Respectfully,
Rufus L. Taylor
Commander, USN.

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
Circular airgram of February 2, 1943 requested the Missions
to bring to the attention of the local government problems
involved in the question of Axis official funds.
Generally the other American Governments were reluc-
tant to block Axis official funds held by the protecting
powers. In some cases, upon our insistence, the local
governments blocked official funds in the respective na-
tional banks.
894,614/10-2345
The Jananese Government, pursuant to a directive from
General MacArthur (Circular airgram of Cctober 15, 1945)
has instructed the protecting powers to turn over the Japan-
ese nroperties an d other assets to the representatives of
the Four Allied Powers. The question has therefore been
raised whether, in cases where local governments have blocked
official Axis funds, they should be permitted to hold custody
or required to transfer them to the Four Allied Powers.
Resclutions 18 and 19 of the Mexico City conference provide
that blocked Axis assets shall be held in status quo until
the American governments unilaterally or by agreement reach
a decision as to their disnosal.
It has been suggested that in as much as Jananese
official funds represent small amounts, of.no great conse-
quence as far as renarations are concerned, and in view of
Cur past position in requesting control by local Governments,
we vill not ask the local governments to transfer custody to
the representatives of the Four Allied Powers. It is con-
temnlated that the whole subject of ultimate disposition of
Axis assets will be raised at the Inter-American Social and
Economic Council pursuant to Resolutions 18 and 19 of the
Hexico City Conference.

were drafted in line vith he foregoing,for clearance.

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 1, despatch no. 3364 dated April 17, 1946,
from the American Embassy in Rome
MEM OR A N D UM
With reference to the recent conversations between
Mr. Doulian of the Soviet Embassy and Mr. Jones of this
Embassy regarding the disposition of Japanese diplomatic
and consular archives and property in Italy, the views of
the Government of the United States on various aspects of
this problem will be of interest to the Soviet Embassy.
1) With respect to procedure to be followed at the
various capitals throughout the world, in receiving
Japanese diplomatic and consular archives and property,
the necessary action should be a matter for decision by
the diplomatic representatives of the four powers (U.S.S.R.,
U.S.A., Great Britain and China) in each capital. In the
event all four of these powers are not represented, the
necessary action will be taken by the diplomatic agents
of those powers which are represented in each capital.
Arrangements will be made in the light of local conditions
and therefore it is not contemplated that the United States
representative must in every case act on behalf of the
other powers.
Consequently the United States Embassy in Rome agrees
with the proposal contained in the Soviet Embassy's note
of December i0, 1945, that the Japanese archives and property
in Italy be placed at the common disposition of the diplo-
matic representatives of the U.S.A., the U.S.S.R., and
Great Britain provided that, however, the representative
of China in Rome be included in this group.
2) Regarding disposition of the property, generally
speaking the United States Government favors the sale of
official, that is, Government owned, property, furniture
and equipment but opposes the sale of the private property
in Rome of Japanese officials. The United States Embassy
has asked for more specific instructions on this point
and any further views of the United States Government in
this regard will be communicated to the Soviet Embassy
when they are received.
3) Regarding the examination of Japanese archives, the
United States Embassy is prepared to furnish immediately
its share of a team of investigators who can read Japanese.
It would be appreciated if the Soviet Embassy would indicate
whether it desires or is prepared to furnish examiners con-
versant with the Japanese language to participate in the
examination of the archives of the former Japanese Embassy,
and if so, when they will be available.
Rome, March 25, 1946

=== Page 294 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclasure no. &, despatch no. 3364 dated April 17, 1946,
from the American Embassy in-Rome
(Translation)
NOTE
VERBALE
The Embassy of the Soviet Union presents its compli-
ments to the Embassy of the United States of America in
Italy and with reference to the memorandum of the Embassy
of the United States of America dated March 25, 1946, regard-
ing the disposition of the Japanese diplomatic and consular
property and archives in Italy has the honor to advise that
it has no objection to the inclusion of the Chinese Represen-
tative among the persons disposing of the above mentioned
property and archives.
As to the segregation of members of the Soviet Union
Embassy knowing the Japanese language for the examination
of the Japanese archives in Italy the Embassy advises that
it cannot segregate any such members having none at the
disposition of the Embassy.
Moreover the Embassy of the Soviet Union bas the honor
to propose to the Embassy of the United States of America
that specialists of the United States of America together
with specialists of the Chinese Embassy in Italy, the Embassy
of the United Kingdom in Italy and the Representative of
the Embassy of the Soviet Union in Italy pick out the most
important documents in order that these documents be later
on photographed and photocopies distributed among all the
four representatives of the allied Powers concerned.
Rome, April 6, 1946

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 3, despatoh no. 3364 dated April 17, 1946,
frem- the American: Embassy in Rome.
The Embassy of the United States of America
presents its compliments to the Embassy of the Soviet
Union and has the honor to refer to its Note Verbale
of April 6, 1946, in which it is stated that the
Embassy of the Soviet Union has no objection to the
inclusion of the representative in Rome of the Chinese
Government among the persons disposing of Japanese
diplomatic and consular property and archives in Italy
and that the Embassy of the Soviet Union has no one
available for examination of the Japanese archives in
Italy with a knowledge of the Japanese language.
With reference to the archives in the Japanese
Embassy, as soon as the inventory of the contents of
the villa, now being prepared by the Allied military
authorities in Rome, has been completed, the American
team of investigators will examine all the archives,
classify them and make the results of their examination
available to this Embassy and the Embassies of the
Soviet Union, China and Great Britain. The representa-
tives of the four Embassies can at that time decide
which categories of documents they consider most impor-
tant and make arrangements to have them photographed or
microfilmed and copies distributed to the four Embassies.
The Embassy of the United States of America would welcome
the collaboration of the representative of the Soviet
Embassy in the examination of the archives to the extent
he considers desirable, and wishes to assure the Soviet
Embassy that its representative is free to visit the
premises of the former Japanese Embassy at any time to
keep himself informed of the progress of the examination
of the archives.
Rome, April 17, 1946

British Embassy, Rome
Chinese Embassy, Rome

SOME FACTS IN FORLER JAPANESE MINISTER YAMAGATA'S DIARY (COnTA)
1942
Aug. 1. The "plan of organized intelligence" wnich ought to
be presented to Gaimucho is very difficult to finish. Prepared
the case of Perú, which will be diseussed tomorrow with the For-
eign Minister.
Sent an intelligence cable.
Aug. 2. Interviewed Foreien Minister about the matter of Peru.
Mr. Nanyo, a Japanese photographer, came to talk with me.
Aug. 4. r. uneeus invited me to tea. The Paraguayan Minister
und new Chilean Minister to Para uay were present. They told me
that President Rfos's attitude is very weak.
Called on Swedish Chargé d'Affaires, and talked about the mat-
ter of S.S. GRIPSHOIM.
Aug. 6. Mr. H. came at noon, and talked about the matter of
Waldo Frank.
Aug. 7. The President of the Senate offered a tea party today.
We had a lot of talk. My impression was that the standpoint of
the Chilean Government is very weak.
Aug. 9. e won at Solomon Sea engagement.
Mr. GA came at 5 P.M. and talked about the speech on rupture
by the Minister of the Interier. But the speech was given in
the presence of President Rios. hat was the real reason then?
Either the Minister of the Interior was trying to make himself
important by ziving this speech or the U.S. President is forcing
them to break relations with the Axis.
Aug. 10. Went to Italian Counselor Migone's house to lunch.
The Chief of Intelligence Dent. Mr. Infante and his wife were
present also.
Ment to mourn the death of Prosiient Rfos's mother and was re-
ceived by the President.
aur. 1l. Met Mr. Nomura, who was attenuing to the business of
Tapanese remittance.
Aug. 12. Hr. Riveros (?) came to tell me that he will leave
ror B.A. by the south, because Panarra refused him plane passage.
Aug. 13. At the request of German Ambassador, called on him and
discussed matter of correspondence. On my way back called on
Italian Ambassador, conferred about the same matter.
Ment to German Embassy to meet Mr. Bohlen. Talked with him about
many things. Mr. Bohlen gave me a fine map.
Received a lot of instructions.
Aug. 14. Called on German Ambassador; asked him about what he
had discussed with Foreign Minister. President Rios will visit
the U.S.A. It's very troublesome.
Aug. 15. Sent cable regarding the President's visit to U.S.A.
Aug。 18.

Aug. 18. Met Spanish Chargé d'Affaires in the afternoon; he
allowed me to intervene in the Colombian case.
Aug. 19. At the request of the German Ambassador, went to see
him at his office at 9 A.M., and talked about many things.
Received important instructicns from Gaimusho at 5 P.M.
Listened to the verbal reports about the steel production of
U.S.A. by the members of Japanese commercial agencies.
Aug. 20. Called on the German Ambassador in the morning, told
him about the instructions which we received yesterday. The Ger-
man Ambassador was satisfied with the content of these instruc-
tions. But when I called on the Italian Ambassador on my way back
he was opposed to the demands. The opinions of Germany and Italy
were usually different.
Acted on the instruction. It's saiu that Mr. Miyasaki, manager
of MiTsUI, was arrested and will be sent to Los Andes to be im-
prisoned, and asked me to help. ent to see the Director of Dip-
lomatic Dept. immediately. The irector allowed me to help.
But Mr. Miyasaki phoned me that he will be sent to prison at 8
tonight. ent to ask the Vice linister of Foreign Affairs again
for help. At 7 P.M. went personally to Police Headquarters,
talked with the section chief in charge of Foreign Affairs. But
he told me that it can't be helped if there was no special per-
missin from the Minister of the Interior. Then I tried to phone
the Minister of the Interior, but unfortunately he was attending
the sessin of Con ress. So I went immediately to the Congress.
By the kindness of Adjutant of the President of Senate, he
handed a small paper to the Iinister of the Interior about my
request. The Minister of the Interior wrote me a note asking
me to go back and granting me an audience with him tomorrow.
By bringing this paper to the Police Headquarters, Mr. Miyasaki
was saved. The Secretary of the Foreign Minister phoned me that
the Minister was helping this case.
Aug. 2l. Interviewed  inister of the Interior in the afternoon.
He did not speak English and was unwilling to receive me with
an interpreter. He is a hateful man. We conversed about an hour.
On my way back, called on Director, Mr. Melo. I then decided
to interview the President, and asked the Chief of Protocol to
arrange a special audience with him. The first appointment
given me was next Tuesday; I considered this too late and finally
arranged one for 1l A.M. tomorrow. When I was interviewing the
Minister of the Interior I had the i ea to ask to speak directly
with the Head of the Secret Service, Mr. Garretón, but later
changed my mind, because I was afraid it might, on the contrary,
make our situation more difficult.
Aug. 22. Interviewed the President, with Interpreter Mr. Naga-
mine. Conversed about 20 minutes. Although the President ex-
pressed his sympathy for the case, it was useless.
Aug. 25. Sent cable about the expulsion of Mr. Miyasaki and other
cables.
Carried out en important instruction.
Aug. 25. Called on the German Ambassador, talked about the ex-
pulsion of Miyasaki and asked him for help. The Ambassador agreed
to entreat Vice Minister of the Interior and other important men
for help.
Ordered Nagamine to get in touch with the German Secretary and
to tell him about the interview with the President.

Mr. Togo was lucky, as was Mr. Nishi.
Went to the Ministry for Foreign Affairs with Nagamine. But Mr.

Mr. X came to tell me about "The visit to the States." He came

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
MAY 141946
FOREIGN ACTIVITY CORRELATION
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
5.28
May 3, 1946
SPD - Mr. Clattenburg:
The memorandum of SPD April 8, 1946 concerning the proposed
revision of the MacArthur Directive dated October 23, 1945 on
894.414/5-346
Japanese official property has been carefully studied by FC and
the following is submitted:
FC agrees that the MacArthur Directive of October 23, 1945
stated that the Four Allied Fowers were to assume joint custody
of official Japanese archives and property and the personal be-
longings of former Japanese diplomatic officials. While SPD has
consistently felt that under these terms the Department must insist
that property and archives of the Japanese Government are to be
preserved intact with certain exceptions, it is the view of FC that
the word "custody" as applied to Japanese archives is not to be
construed to mean that such archives are to be maintained or pre-
served indefinitely. The Directive states that "the protecting power
concerned will be instructed by the Japanese Government to turn over
intact and without delay to representatives of the Four Allied
Powers, who have been instructed to receive them, physical custody
of all Japanese diplomatic and consular property and archives in
CS/NS
the country concerned". The Directive thereafter remains silent
on the ultimate disposition of such properties and archives after
they have been turned over intact and without delay. Since there
are no compelling reasons why such archives are to be maintained
indefinitely after it has been determined by satisfactory examination
that they are of no value to this Government such archives need not
be required for permanent preservation since they should be regarded
as captured enemy records by the United states and not as property
of the United States or the sole responsibility of this Government
under whose regulations only United States archives must be preserved
8
as public records. As title to Japanese archives is vested in the
94.414
Four Allied Powers, it is therefore the responsibility of the Four
Allied Powers as to whether such records should be preserved or
destroyed.
The Department heretofore has issued instructions in a circu-
lar airgram of January 31, 1946 indicating that requests will not
5
be entertained from the Mission for destruction of any part of the
archives. At that time it was felt that the Department would be
1
G
able to obtain teams of examiners for dispatch to missions abroad.
46
As the
D1=

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
As the Department has not yet been able to obtain Japanese experts,
the Missions accordingly have been advised to arrange for local
examination of the archives (e.g. Buenos Aires, Bogota, Lima).
In such instructions the Department has also indicated that Japanese
archives are to be maintained and preserved for access thereto or
for further investigation (e.g. Buenos Aires, Brussels). Results
of the examinations of some Missions indicate that nothing of value
was found in the records and the Department has been advised that
Japanese representatives had ample opportunity to destroy records
of value (Stockholm, Beirut).
In the circumstances, FC suggests that the question is not
one of amending or modifying the acArthur Directive, but rather
it is a question of the interpretation of the terms of the Directive
on the U. S. Government. It is felt that this whole guestion should
now be reexamined in the light of the policy followed by the other
three Governments in order to reach a common understanding with
representatives of the Four Allied Powers in the ultimate disposition
of the properties nd archives. FC is of the opinion that when
satisfactory examination of the archives has been achieved they
should in agreement with representatives of the Four Allied Powers
be destroyed or otherwise disposed of. On the matter of properties
and liquid assets FC concurs in the suggestion of SPD.
20
Frederick B. Lyon,
Chief, Division of Foreign
Activity Correlation.

1. Amuassauor to the Holy See, Ken Harada. As reported
previously, Mr. Harada had been in the service of the League of
Naticns until he was taken in the Japanese diplomatic service
following the Japanese withdrawal from the Le ue. He was one
of the persons who I hoped would be willing to collaborate with
the Allies for democratization of Japan. However, while discuss-
ing with myself aboari the ss Plus Uitra on the posrible colla-
boration petween the Allies and Japan, Mr. Harada expressed the
opinion that such. colla oration would hardly help Japan. He
stated that the best policy any Japanese patriot could follow
at this moment might be NoT to collaoorate with, nor offend, the
Occupation Authorities an that the Occupation troops would get
tired of the occupation before lons years or might be called for
more urgent tasks in a theacer other than the Japanese, when
Japan mieht oe able to o her own way. He expressed the fear
that any member of the Japanese government in collaboration with
the Occupation Authorities mi it be treated as traitors after
the Occupation troops are withdrawn as they should be one day.
To his mind, non-coilaboration would brine about a situation under
which the Occupation Authorities could see any way out but quit
Japan, democratic or totalitarian.
2. Ambassador in Turkey, Kurihara. He is of the group 1ed
by Ex-Ambassador Shiratori, now a war criminal. He stated that
the Allied au thorities with their proposed collaboration with the
Japanese mi it try to apnease the vanquished who mi ht be useful
for the Allied purposes in a not distant future, but that such ap-
peasement had been offerel too late. "The Allies went too far
for any Japanese patriot to come to terms with them," he declared.
His information officers were divided on the subject. Mr. Morio
Aoki, second secretary of the Japanese embassy in Turkey, who
was in char e of information services in Ankara, did not believe
in the usefulness of collaboration, vhile Mr. Izumi, another
second secretary of the same embassy, who was in charge of infor-
mation services in Istanbul, was in favor of collaboration.
3. Ambassadors Hidaka and Mitani. Both of them refrained

in Japan. Both of them were critized by his subordinates for

CONFIDENTIAL
Prior to his departure from Colombo, General Onodera has
charged me to transmit to Mr. Adolf J. Lium a statement (writ-
ten in Japanese) whose translation is as follows:-
A)
(1) Since 0. had taken up his post in Stockholm, he has
always done his best for making use of the German information
services for his own purposes, as it seemed to be the best
policy he could rollow under the circumstances. Among the
German officers who collaborated with him most closely are
Col. von Heimann, air attache, and Dr. K. H. Kraemer. The
former passed over to 0. the information obviously gathered
in Berlin. O. can not figure out if K. also passed over in-
formation originated from Berlin sources or information col-
lected by K. himself, who did not disclose his sources.
(2) In exchange for their information, the Germans were
given "little" information (quotation is M.E.'s.) received by
0.'s own service as well as other information received from
his colleagues in Spain, Switzerland, and Portugal, who had
been in close contact with himself. Especially since communi-
cations between Berlin and Tokio were made difficult during
the last months of the war, O. had quite many things to tell
to the German collaborators on the basis of information he got
directly from Tokio.
(3) As it was part of his duties at Stockholm to watch the
German situation for objective appraisal, he had had difficulty
in dealin with Germans on more than one occasion.
(4) Information he got on the Western Front was mostly of
the German sources. He did not gather as much information on
the Eastern Front, in accordance with Tokio's policy.
(5) Collaboration with non-German Allied officers.
a) no collaboration with Italians.
b) collaboration with Finnish military Attache, Mr.
Stewens, had been arranged for information service in the East.
He found, however, that S, did not know very much of the Red
Army and was too friendly toward the Soviets to pass objective
judgement on the Soviet situation.
c) Hungarian military attache, Lt.-Col. Kobor seemed
to be a "good fellow" as far as personal contact is concerned
but was not very useful for information service. His assistants,
first Mr. Vagi and later Mr. Voezikoendy, are among the most
capable officers O. came in contact for information service in
Europe. Vagi is at once anti-German and anti-Soviet. He is
liked by many Swedes, with whom he came in contact in social
affairs and were wiliing to help him (v.) in his service. As
he is not a Russian specialist, however, it is doubtful that
V. will be
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
V. will be successful in organising a Russian information
service under his own direction. Voezikoendy had little time
to show his capacity in Stockholm, but O. personally knows
that Voezi. was very successful in organising the Russian in-
formation service of the Budapest General Staff. He may be
found useful in the future as he seemed to be friend of the
Western Powers.
B)
According to O.'s own experiences, it is very difficult
for Japanese to organise in Europe an information net-work of
born-Japanese agent. It seemed most fruitful to single out
European agents with former record at Japanese offices for a
Japanese-managed net-work according to their respective capa-
city and objectives and further to organise close collabora-
tion with friendly European services or individual members
thereßf. O. could have certain success in services organised
along the above lines.
(1) Balts. O. has been able to find a mumber of capable
and useful collaborators from among many Baltic emigrants in
Sweden and Germany. Some of them were in German information
services. Information he received from Baltic collaborators
has often proved to be accurate and useful. Baltic collabora-
tors are very capable in Russian information service. In one
case, a Baltic collaborator was very successful for French
information service, and his information, when passed over to
the German service, was highly appreciated by Kraemer. (0.
apparently refers to Messing. -ME.)
(2) Finns seem to have developed the best information
service in Europe as far as the Soviet codes are concerned.
O. does not think that the Finnish service in Russia
is very extensive but that it is most intensive and gathers
accurate information about details. He has reason to believe
that Finns have certain preparations for sabotage in Russia.
(3) Poles have developed services most useful for Russian
information and most powerful for underground work inaide
Russia. Many Polish officers are friendly toward Japanese,
and some of them are most trustworthy friends of Japanese in-
formation services. During the war, however, full collabora-
tion between the Polish and Japanese services had not been
organised until the German capitulation, partly because the
Japanese avoided offendin their German aily by linking them-
selves closely with Poles and partly because the Poles did
not want to help Germans through their information which might
be passed over to Germans via Japanese. Provi ded the Russian
policy of the Western Powers is made clear in one way or
another, the Polish friends of the Japanese services will be
ready to accept a Japanese proposal for tripartite collabora-
tion among the Western Powers, Poland and Japan for informa-
tion service.
Polish services in London, Buoarest, and Istanbul
could be contacted via Japanese channel efrectively, as lead-
ing officers at the three posts of Polish observation have
special links of confidence with 0.
(4) Swedes.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
(4) Swedes. O. failed in organizing Swedish communists
and social democrats for his infommation purposes. The Swedish
military information service as a whole was not very collabora-
tive because the Swedish General Staff was afraid of Russian
reaction to collaboration with the Japanese. Som individual
members of Swedish service, however, have been contacted
(mainly through social contacts).
(5) Hungarians. Col. Hidai of the Hungarian General Staff
is one of the most capable coordinators of Russian information
O. met in Europe. However, he might not be very successful
organizer of information service in Russia. The latter job may
be iven Voezikoendy and Lt.-Col. Janos Andreanszky with greater
chance of success. Vai may be useful for supplementary service
of Russian informationin Sweden.
0. has added that he is only ready to answer any question
by Mr. Lium or his appointee for further elucidation on the aove
statement.

Subject: Japanese Archives in Erussels.
894.414/6-346
The Ambassador has the honor to refer to the Department's
airgram No. A-130 of April 25, 1?46 wherein the Embassy was
requested to submit additional information on the archives of the
former Japanese Embassy in russels.
There is enclosed herewith a transcription of the index of
pro-Japanese and anti-Japanese persons found in the Japanese
archives (enclosure No. 1), as well as a list of the titles of the
cinema fiIms found in the Japanese Embassy (enclosure No. 2). As
of further interest to the Department, there is enclosed a list
of persons who are believed to be employees of the former Japanese
Embassy (enclosure No. 3).
Enc osures:
(1)
List of pro-Japanese and anti-Japanese.
(2)
List of titles of the cinema films.
(3) List of persons believed to be Japanese Embassy employees.

No. 219
RESTRICTED
Brussels, June 3, 1946.
Subject: Japanese Archives in Brussels.
The Ambassador has the honor to refer to the Department's
airgram No. A-130 of April 25, 1946 wherein the Embassy was
requested to submit additional information on the archives of the
former Japanese Embassy in Brussels.
There is enclosed herewith a transcription of the index of
pro-Japanese and anti-Japanese persons found in the Japanese
archives (enclosure No. 1), as well as a list of the titles of the
cinema films found in the Japanese Embassy (enclosure No. 2). As
of further interest to the Department, there is enclosed a list
of persons who are believed to be employees of the former Japaness
Embassy (enclosure No. 3).
Enc osures:
(1)
List of pro-Japanese and anti-Japanese.
(2)
List of titles of the cinema films.
(3)
List of persons believed to be Japanese Embassy employees.
RBJordan/eb
To the Department in original and copy prepared for ozalid.

CA's memorandum of June 25, 1946 concerning the desire

π

894.414/6

the Japanese Government is, in connection with this specific

down in the directive. Hence, it is highly desireble that the

6-1646

C
without ready access in the areas concerned to the pertinent
information.
1
A

authorities. In addition to possible political aspects, the
treatment accorded Japanese archives elsewhere is involved on
F.W.894.414/6-1646
a precedent basis. The interested divisions of the Department

AG O91.112 (22 May 46) GB
22 May 1946
SUBJECT: Records of Japanese Consulates in China
TO
Chief of Staff, War Department, Washington 25, D. C.
(Attention: Assistant Chief of Staff, G-2.)
1. Reference is made to letter, Japanese Government, Central
Liaison Office, to General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers, Subject: "Request for Permission to bring Consular Court
Documents from China", dated 2 March 1946, attached as an enclosure.
2. It is presumed that the U. S.Army Forces in China have had
or will have access to documents in Japanese Consulates General and the
Japanese Embassy in China. If this is not the case, it is believed that
possibly new intelligence on Japanese operations in China could be
obtained were all Consular Documents delivered to this Headquarters.
3. It is considered that the collection and acquisition of
records of former Japanese Consular and Diplomatic Missions would pre-
sumably be subject to prior agreement with the Government of China on
a governmencal level.
4. It is recommended that necessary action be taken to obtain
the concurrence of the Governnent of China on the return of Japanese
Consular Court Documents to Japan and if deemed appropriate, the
extension of this reguest to include all Japanese Consular Documents.

=== Page 454 === (confidence 0.99)
 IMPERIAL JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
Central Liaison Office
TO
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS OF THE SUPREME COMMANDER
FOR THE ALLIED POWERS.
FROM : Central Liaison Office, Tokyo.
SUBJECT: Request for Permission to Bring Consular Court
Documents from China
C.L.O. No. 971(5.2)
2 March 1946
The under-mentioned documents are kept in custody at the former Japanese
Consulates in China. The Japanese Goverrment re uires them for the purposes
mentioned under the relevant heads, and desires to obtain the permission of
the GenBral Headquarters for bringing them to Japan from China.
(1) Original texts or judgements on civil cases and sentences on
criminal cases.
(2) Protocols of mediation and arbitration.
(3) Registers or various cases, civil and criminal.
(4) Registers and original texts of notarial deeds.
These doc ments are required to provide, e.g., for cases
where adjudications, mediations, arbitrations, notarial
acts, etc. already effected by Consular Courts are to
be executed in Japan in the future, or where such
a
adjudications, mediations, arbitrations, notarial acts,
etc. need to be used as data in relation to future civil
and criminal cases in Japan.
(5) Lists of names of ex-convicts.
These will be necessary in connection with criminal
cases involving Japanese repatriates from China, in
order to ascertain whether ani what previous criminal
records such Japanese repatriates have.
(6) Records of pending cases, civil and criminal.
(7)
 Records relatin to the execution of sentences.
(8) Records of the collection of major fines, minor fines, etc.
The proceedin s in civil and criminal cases and those in
relation to the execution of sentences, which had been in
progress until the war ended, were suspended wich the
termination of the war. These proceedin s will have to
be resumed with the repatriation of th persons concerned.

=== Page 455 === (confidence 0.99)
The above-mentioned documents form the requisite data
for the resumption of such proceedings.
(9) Registers of immovable property and commercial registers.
Registers of immovable property are required as data
bearing on various questions which may arise concerning
the rights pertaining to immovable property hitherto
enjoyed by Japanese in China; and commercial registers,
as data for lawsuits involving Japanese repatriates.
(10)
) Registers of census registration reports received and duplicates of
household reports (both not prior to January 1944).
(11)
The originals of accepted census registration reports which have not
het been sent to Japan.
Some of the census registration reports accepted by the
former Japanese Consulates in China were lost while in
transit to Japan, or were destroyed when the offices of
the Ministry of Greater East Asia were burned in air-
raids. After consultation with the Ministry of Justice
it has been decided that the same particulars as the
contents of the census registration reports so lost or
destroyed shall be entered on the registers of the census
offices at places of domicile, only when the fact that the
reports were accepted by the Consulates is attested to by
by the Ministry ofForeign Affairs. The documents under
(10) are required for the purpose of that attestation,
while those under (1l) are necessary to enable the
census offices concerned to make the re uisite entries.
FOR THE PRESIDENT,
s/ s. Iguchi
t/ s. Iguchi
Director of Ceneral Affairs
Central Liaison Office.

=== Page 456 === (confidence 0.96)

NOE: July 16, 1946 DCk
TO
:SPD - Mr. Hib rd
DEPARTMEN OF STATE
 FROM : CA - Mr. Drumright
suBjEcT:Desire of Japanese authoritie
in China.
CA regrets that it is not in a position to initial the
attached telegram. FE has taken the same view.
In the first place, it is our view that any inquiry on
this subject should be addressed to the Embassy and not to
the Consulate General at Shanghai. If representations of any
kind are to be made to the Chinese Government, they will, of
course, be made through the Embassy. ioreover, the Embassy
is, or ought to be, in a better position to give the Depart-
894.414/
ment a mo overall report on the subject than the Consulate
General at shanghai.
In the second place, CA feels that the first paragraph
of the draft tel gram should be revised to make it conform
as closely as possible to the request of the Central Liaison
office.
In the third place, as CA pointed out in its memorandum
9
of June 25, 1946. American interest in the Japanese documents
is at best of minor importance unless for intelligence purposes
To date CA does not recall a single request on the part of
9
Americans for examination of these Japanese records. It seems
4
to CA, therefore, that we should not base our inquiry on the
9
possible retention of the records in China for American use.
If, as is sug ested in your memorandum of July 9, 1946,
what is wanted is information in regard to the value of the
Japanese records in China, then it seems to CA that the final
C
S
paragraph of your telegram should reflect that desire and
/A
not be restricted to an estimate of the situation based on
American interests.
With reference to the question of American property
F0.894.414/6-1646
interests and their possible transfer to Japanese during the
war, CA's information is to the effect that most if not
all, such properties have been or are in the process of being
returned to their rightful owners through the agency of the
Chinese Alien Property Custodian.
CA

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
CA wishes to suggest that Japanese records in China may
not be compared with Japanese diplomatic or consular archives
seized in other countries. Until 1943, Japan enjoyed
extraterritorial rights in China and the bulk of the records
sought by the Japanese Government appear to relate to that
circumstance. It seems to CA that the Japanese records in
China must needs be retained in China for some time to come in
order to clarify disputed problems. The Chinese authorities
will no doubt wish to have constant recourse to such documents
in order to solve property disputes and make decisions in
reparations nd other related matters. It is CA's view that
the Chinese authorities will not agree to the delivery of the
Japane.e records to the Japanese Government. In our own
case, we pro ose to leave our court and other extraterritorial
records in China until the possibility of their use there is
exhausted.
If SCAP or the Japanese Government have legitimate
reasons for going into the Japanese records in China, it seems
that representatives thereof might be sent to China for that
purpose. While the Chinese authorities will in all probability
refuse to deliver the rocords to the Japanese Government or to
SCAf, they may very likely permit inspection of the records in
China if there is a good reason adduced therefor.
A:DFDrunright:MS

The Minister apparently had reference to the
Embassy's memorandum to the Foreign Office dated August
17, 1945, copy attached, requesting the views of the
Brazilian Government regarding the mostappropriate
manner to proceed in obtaining safe custody of the
Japunese archives and property. There is no record of
a written reply having been received to the Embassy's
memorandum of August 17, 1945; however, the following
quotations are taken from a memorandum in the Embassy 's
files signed by Mr. Paul C. Daniels on November 5, 1945:
"Shortly before 5 P.M. today, I called on
Ambassador Carlos Muniz, Secretary General of
the Foreign Office, by previous appointment to
discuss some routine matters. He was informed
of the Swedish linister's instructions (in fact,
the Swedish Minister was at the Itamarati while
I vas there), but reiterated what he had said
some months ago; that his political advisers
thought it would be more in keeping with precedent
for the Brazilian Foreign Office to be recipient
of the archives (Japanese archives). I said that
our instructions authorized us to accept the archives
but that naturally we wished to avoid any argument
with the Bruzilian Government on the matter. Mr.
Muniz said that he and the Foreign Minister would
consider the Swedish Minister's note and what posi-
tion they would take in the matter, and agreed that
we would defer further discussions for a couple of
days until such time as the interested parties
might informally reach some agreement as to procedure
and avoid, if possible, unnecessary formalities,
notes or nev instructions. In the meantime, an
opportunity will be afforded to reach an agreement
With the British and Chinese regarding further procedure".
It wus finally agreed 1/ that the Brazilian Govern-
ment vould not re uest custody of Jupanese property, but
would be agreeable to a procedure involving merely the
presence of a representative from the Itamarati when the
arehives and property were turned over to the three Em-
basies.
There appeare to be no record in the mbassy files
regurding  desire of Brazil to participate as part owner
in the Jupanese property and funds. However, the

Enclosure to Despatch No.
115 from American Embassy
Rio de Janeiro, Brazil dated
June 28, 1946 entitled
(COPY)
"Japanese Property".
MEMORANDU
The Government of the United States of America,
acting also on behalf of the Governments of Great Britain,
the Soviet Union and China, is desirous of obtaining custody
of Japanese archives and property in belligerent countries
before they can be destroyed. It is understood that the
Japanese Government will authorize the Swedish Legation at
Rio de Janeiro to relinquish Japanese property and archives
now in Brazil. It is likevise understood that the British
and Chinese diplomatic missions in Rio de Janeiro are
receiving instructions in the matter.
The views of the Government of Brazil regarding the
most appropriate manner for proceeding in order to ensure
the safe custody of the Japanese archives and property will
be welcomed by the Embassy.
Rio de Janeiro, August 17, 1945.

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.98)
APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. In carrying out the provisions of the MacArthur
directive, the Department of State has been faced with a
number of practical problems. The directive as it now
stands provides for the trensfer to the Allied Powers of
"physical custody" of Japanese official property. Under
a strict interpret: ion of the term "physical custody"
the Dep rtment of State has been unable to agree to pro-
posals, made in some cases by its own representatives and
in others by representatives of the Allied Powers concerned,
that certain properties be sold or destroyed at this time,
even though the Department agrees that such disposition
would be hi hiy desirable from a practical standpoint.
Instanecs where sale or destruction is considered advisable
or even imperetive fall roughly into three categories:
a. Propeganda mateiel (i.e. stamps, insigne,
flags, etc.,) ar necessarily bein: preserved in
storage althou n they are of no practical use and
present a potential danger from a political viewpoint.
b. In many cases official movable erfects are
bein, stored et considerable expense with a consequent
lessenins of tre total value of the property being
held br the Allied Powers. Furthermore, in many cases
official property is being withheld from sale or use
although it is situated in countries where there is an
acute shorta e of consumer's goods.
c. Altheuh it is agreed that wherever possible
property belonging to former Japanese diplomatic and
consular personnel shoule be kept intact for possible
return to the owners thereof, exception should be made
in the

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.96)
2.
-2 -
in the case of perishable goods (such as wines)
or items vith a rapidly depreciating value (such as
automobiles), where conversion into cash at this
time would clearly be of benefit to the owners.
2. It should  noted that any change in the status of
official property does not imply any dispositon of the property
other than conversion into cash wii ch will continue to be held
by the representatives of the Allied Powers for ultimate dis-
position as determined by reparations agreement. The only
other disposition contemplated is the destruction of certain
useless and potentially tangerous articles.
3. There has been a certain divergence of opinion among
the Four Allied Powers in interpretin: the Cetober 25, 1945
directive. The t.S. position has consistently been to interpret
literally the phrase "physical custody" which precludes any
disposition whatever of property or archives. The other powers
nave on occasion relt thet the needs of the situation would
best be served b, sellin certain items, notably movable pro-
perty, or have in several cases surgested final disposition by
means of a quadripertite division oi either physical property
or funds. his is entirely contrary to the esbablished position
of tuis overnment and is is considered desirable thet any
modificetion of the  resent directive be exyre ssed in such
language as to prevent any such interpretation.

=== Page 487 === (confidence 0.99)
APPENDIX "C"
CONCLUSIONS
1. The following paragraphs are suggested for addition
to the MacArthur directive to th Japanese Government of
October 25, 1945 in order to facilitate agreement between the
Four Allied Fowers regarding Japanese diplomatic and consular
property and archives:
(E) Title to the official property of Jp anese
Government which was turned over to the Four Allied
Fowers is vested in those Powers which are authorized
in their discretion to liquidate movable properties;
the proceeds therefrom together with all other property
to
should be held for disposition in accordance with pans.
agreements.
(F) The provisions of paragraph E with respect to
the disposition of official Japanese property will not
be held to apply to diplomatic and consular archives of
the Japanese Government which will in all instances be
preserved intact. The term "archives" shall be construed
to cover ofricial documents, consular registers, and
such correspondence as may pertain to Japanese nationals
living abroad. The term "archives" shall not be construed
to cover propaganda material, libraries, stamps, seals,
flags, insigne, and similar items which may be destroyed
in agreement with representatives of the Four Allied Powers.
(G) As custodians of property of former Japanese
diplomatic and consular personnel the Four Powers have
authority to take advantage of available opportunities
for sale of such properties where the value thereof can
only be preserved by conversion into cash.

AIR MAIL

MEMORANDUM
EXAMINATION OF THE ARCHIVES FOUND IN HE JA PANESE LEGATION
Mexico City, Mexico.
The examination of the Japanese Archives and Mis-
cellaneous Papers of the former Japanese Legation at
Mexico City, began on Thursday, April 25, 1946, at the
American Embassy in Mexico City, Mexico. It ended on
June 25, 1946, with the showing of four reels of 35
centimeter film, 672 meters in length entitled, SERVICE
IN THE JAPANESE NAVY, found with the Miscellaneous Papers o
of the Japanese Legation. The film is dated about 1936,
and is of no political Importance. Its intent, propaganda
purposes only.
Mr. Tien Yuen Chang, First Secretary of the Chinese
Embassy; Mr. Grigori Kasparov, First Secretary of the
Russian Embassy; Mr. Edward Osborne Coote, First Secretary
of the British Embassy; and Mr. Walter Linthicum, Second
Secretary of the Empassy of the United States of America,
conducted the examination of the Archives and of the Mis-
cellaneous Papers under reference. Messrs. Chang and
Kasparov translated all Japanese documents to the English
language for the benefit of Mr. Coote and Mr. Linthicum.
The Archives for the sake of order and convenience,
were lettered before examination by volumes A to L, in-
clusive. Other bound volumes found in the Miscellaneous
Papers, for the same reasons, were numbered from I to
XIII, inclusive. The Miscellaneous Papers were scattered
loosely in one small and two large cases. To facilitate
handling such Papers they were placed in used manila fold-
ers. The archives were examined first, then the bound
volumes of Miscellaneous Papers and lastly the loose Mis-
cellaneous Papers and bound books containing copies of
telegrams received and sent by the Japanese Legation at
Mexico City during the period from 1894 to 1922. The
following is a report on what, of interest, was found in
the material examined:
ARCHIVES
BOOK A
Book A contains certificates of citizenship and letters
of guarantee issued to Japanese nationals crossing from
Mexico into the United States during the period from June 2,
1934, to March 1, 1938. Other letters found in this volume
showed that Japanese social societies in Mexico freouently
wrote to the Japanese Legation guaranteeing certain indi-
viduals as to their responsibility and citizenship.
Book B

=== Page 491 === (confidence 0.99)
4
- 2-
BOOK B
Book B is a record of vital statistics from the
period of February 23, 1939, to September 12, 1944. It
is divided into sections reading as follows:
a.- Official Correspondence (in which there
appears no correspondence);
b.- Marriage;
c.- Divorce;
d.- Expatriation;
e.- The loss of nationality;
f.- Births and deaths.
Certain letters in this file indicate there were in
Mexico Japanese social associations and especially one,
ASOCIACION JAPONESA at Piedras Negras, Coahuila. The
letter pertains to Japanese nationalization.
BOOK C
Book C consists of Certificates in the Japanese
language of Miscellaneous Matters divided into sections
"S", "T", and "U".
Under Section "S" of this Volume, which covers
the period from January 17, 1939, to November 19,
1941, there is nothing of political importance.
Section "T" covers the period from October 30,
1940, to May 6, 1941. These records show that in
1939 the fishing vessel "NOHI MARU" went aground
in the Bay of California when bound from Guaymas,
and that seven other Japanese fishing vessels ans-
wered its call for help. Such is mentioned here
only to give an idea of the large number of Japanese
fishing vessels in the area at the time.
Section "U" covers the period from January 1,
1939, to October 6, 1941. Apparently before oil
could be shipped to Japan a certificate in the Japanese
language as to the amount and destination of the oil
had to be obtained from a Japanese consular official.
The records show that the following suoh certificates
were issued by the Japanese Consulate General at
Mexico City and the dates stated:
l.- Pacific Petroleum Company, November
6, 1939. Amount of oil shipped not stated;
2.- Pacific Petroleum Company, November
15, 1939. Amount of oil shipped not stated,
but it is indicated that the oil was for the
Japanese Navy;
3.- Dacific Petroleum Company, December
8, 1939. Shipment of some 87,878.23 barrels
of crude oil (42 gallons each)was made on the
Motor Vessel "HUZISIAN MARU" on December 3
and 4, 1939. It was loaded at Tuxpan, Veracruz,
and consigned to the Pacific Petroleum Company
at Tokuyama, Japan;
400

=== Page 492 === (confidence 0.97)
4
2
-3-

BOOK E
Book E concerns Applications for Validation of
Japanese Passports and Applications for Visas to Enter
or Pass in Transit through Japan. June 6, 1936 to
October 20, 1941.
Under the section Applications for Visas for Aliens
to Enter or Pass Through Japan of this Volume, June 6,
1939, to September 12, 1941, there are a great many ap-
plications for visas filed by Germans in 194l, to return
to Germany via Japan, because of the war then in Europe.
All such applications were made before the United States
officially entered the war on December 7, 194l. One such
applicant was Clemens Schuck, who stated in his applica-
tion that he was born in Brooklyn, New York, on June 20,
1919, but was the bearer of German passport No. 3275,
dated August 26, 1940.

BOOK G
Book G contains Applications for Japanese Passports
from January 20, 1939, to October 20, 1941.
All applications in this Volume are in the Japanese

Most of the applicants were proceeding through the United

BOOK H
Book H contains Correspondence with the Diplomatic
and Consular Corps, dating from February 22, 1933, to
October 17, 1941.
This Volume contains miscellaneous correspondence,
mostly in the Spanish language, indexed alphabetically
under each country and pertaining principally to national
holidays, assumptions of office, letters of condolence
and invitations to official functions.
BOOK I
Book I deals with Correspondence with Mexican Depart-
ment of the Interior Regarding Japanese Nationals Entering
Mexico. January 4, 1934 to August 22, 1941.
The correspondence in this Volume pertains to the
entry of certain Japanese into Mexico, and their registra-
tion as residents of Mexico. On December 2, 1941, accord-
ing to letters in this Volume, the Domei News Agency, 50
Rockefeller Plaza, New York City, applied to have their
representative, a Mr. Kuno, enter Mexico as correspondent.
The Japanese Legation here replied it was best that the
correspondent should not cone to Mexico at that time.
BOOK J
Book J contains Demographic Certificates dating
from February 4, 1939, to April 14, 1941.
This Volume contains petitions in the Japanese
language for permission to have certain relatives join
the applicant in Mexico. It contains further the
genealogy of certain Japanese families, apparently then
residents of Mexico.

no political importance.

VOLUME II

=== Page 497 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
10
VOIIME II
INCOMING AND OUTGOING TELEGRAMS
1923
Volume II is a large loose-leaf volume which
contins true readings, mostly in the Japanese language,
of incoming and outgoing telegrams sent in code. A
perusal of the book indicates that it was the policy
of the Japanese Foreign Office to keep its Embassies
informed by telegraph of all important matters connoeted
with the foreign relations of Japan and other important
subjects in which other than the Japanese nation was
interested. This makes the volume valuable from a
historic point of view. There are many telegrams re-
garding the earthquake in Japan in 1923; telegrams in
1923 regarding the re-establishment of Russo-Japanese
diplomatic relations; Japanese fishing rights off the
coast of KAMCHATKA; a telegram dated August 31, 1923,
wherein the Japanese Embassy in Mexico reports to Tokyo
the recognition of the new Mexican Government by the
United States; a copy of a telegram from the Japanese
Embassy in Washington to Tokyo dated January 27, 1923,
stating that the Pan American Union and the League of
Nations could not work together; a telegram dated
January 13, 1923, from the Japanese Foreign Office in-
structing the Japanese Ambassador to investigate and report
on economic activities of the United States in Mexico;
and a telegram from the Japanese Embassy in Mexico to
the Japanese Foreign Office reporting on matters re-
lated to the PURISINA oil company, in which a person
named Gomez primarily was interested. Later telegrams
indicate that the PURISINA oil company was an explor-
ing company which did not find oil in Mexico. It is
apparent that the Japanese then were interested in
having oil holdings in Mexico.
VOLUME III
(Labeled) RICARDO MATUSITA.
July 3, 1939, to May 14, 1940.
The person named above formerly was an employee
of the Japanese Legation in Mexico City, who later
became engaged in selling a washing powder and Japanese
toilet articles. It is a commercial matter that has no
political significance. However, there was found in
1/
this volume a letter dated May 26, 1941, addressed to
Señorita Elena Cornejo signed by the Economic Counselor
of the American Embassy at the time, Mr. Thomas H.
21
Lockett. The letter of Miss Cornejo requesting the in-
formation dated May 19, 1941, was also in this file.
On February 6, 1941, Mr. Lockett also wrote a letter to
Mr. Matusita, at his written request, giving him the
name of certain merchants in Massachussetts and Wisconsin.

VOLUME IX

True readings of telegrams, mostly originating in

Japanese Embassy in Washington to the Japanese Legation
in Mexico City. Of interest was found the following:
(a) A telegram dated January 1, 1924, from the
Japanese mbassy in Washington to their establish-
ment in Mexico City, stating that according to
newspaper reports the United States Government
agreed to sell to the Obregon Government lo to
15 thousand Springfield rifles and l0 million
rounds of ammunition, and l0 airplanes;
(b)
On January 6, 1924, another telegram sent by
the Jananese Ambassador in Washington reporting
to Tokyo that according to his information the
United States Secretary of Wer had informed
him that certain sales or armaments had been
made to the kiexican Government;
(c)
Immediately following the above message are other

on the exportation of arms from the United States
to mexico;
(d)
Other telegrams relate to the anti-American
feeling in Japan due to the rassing of the Immi-
gretion Act of 1924, one of which is dated June
9. 1924. It is interesting to note that in some
instances, and no doubt on purpose, the Japanese
sent their messages in the Inglish lenguage on
non-confiden tiel mauters related to their national
poliey.

=== Page 500 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
13
VOLUME X
TRUE READINGS BETWEEN JAPANESE DIPLOMATIC MISSIONS
OF TELEGRAMS IN THE JAPANESE LANGUAGE
1924
In this volume is a message, dated May 18, 1924, sent
by the Japanese Ambassador at Rio de Janeiro to the Japanese
representative in Mexico City outlining the policy of the
Japanese as to the Immigration Act of 1924.  There are
other messages in the volume pertaining to this same Im-
migration Act, all of which show the feelings of the
Japanese against the United States at that time.
Another telegram in Volume X is dated October 1,
1924, and refers to a survey made in Mexico. The survey
relates to a plan to settle some 20,ooo Japanese on farms
in Mexico, especially in the states of Nayarit, Sinaloa
and Colima. A message dated October 28, 1924, from the
Japanese Consul in Los Angeles to the Japanese Legation
in Mexico also refers to the wholesale igration of
Japanese into Mexico. The latter message mentions that
the American newspapers had contained articles to the ef-
fect that 20,Ooo Japanese were to settle in Mexico, in
cluding two members of the Japanese General Staff and some
ten Japanese financiers. The Japanese Legation in Mexico
City denied that such a plan was in effect, but other
telegrams in this Volume clearly indicate that the Japanese
Government was behind such a plan, and that one Tobasi
worked very hard to put the plan into operation.
Other telegrams show that the Japanese Minister to
Mexico negotiated with the Mexican Government to permit
the settlement of agricultural Japanese in this country,
and had received the approval of his plan in principle
from the Mexican authorities. However, there is nothing
in this Volume to indicate that the plan ever was put into
effect. The plan apparently was conceived after the en-
actment of the Immigration Act of 1924 which excluded
Japanese in numbers entering the United States. The
Japanese were particularly interested in settling in Mexico
along the Pacific Coast.
VOLUME XI
TRUE READINGS OF TELEGRAMS IN THE JAPANESE LANGUAGE
BETWEEN JAPANESE DIPLOMATIC MISSIONS
1925
The only telegram in this volume of interest, and
then from a historical view only is one dated June 10,
1925. In this message the Japanese Ambassador in Wash-
ington advised his colleague in Mexico City that Mr.
Kellogg wanted to maintain friendly relations with Mexico,
but that he was very much dissatisfied with the Mexican
agrarian law whereby some American citizens had had their
property

=== Page 501 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
14
property confiscated. Other messages in this book relate
to the same subject, including one long telegram wherein
the Japanese Ambassador in Washington gives his opinion
that the Mexican agrarian law will not lead to serious
differences between the United States and Mexico.

VOLUME XIII
TRUE READINGS OF TELEGRAMS MOSTLY ORIGINATING
IN TOKYO, REPEATED BY THE JAPANESE EMBASSY, WASHING-
TON, TO THE JAPANESE LEGATION IN MEXICO CITY.
1926
There were no messages in this volume of political
or other interest. Most of the telegrams were for back-
ground, originating in the Japanese Foreign Office. One
such message in this book relates to the formation of the
National News Agency in Japan, sponsored by eight Japanese
newspapers. Another message from the Japanese Ambassador
in Washington states the New York Times had published that
Colonel R.A. Vasquez had consented to grant Dr. Osawa (now
a veterinary surgeon in Mexico City) oil and rubber concessions
on some 200 acres of land in Mexico, and advised the Japanese
Minister in Mexico City to confirm the rumor.

fidential instruction got into the Japanese files is
naturally rather surprising.
The Miscelleanous Papers of the Japanese Embassy
include further some 25 bound books in which were re-
corded telegrams received and sent by the Japanese
Legation at Mexico City during the period from 1894 to
1922. A perusal of these books indicates that in the
early years most all the messages were in the English
language, and that they are of a historical value only.
As previously mentioned elsewhere in this memorandum,
the Japanese Foreign Office endeavored to keep its
foreign emissaries informed by telegraph as to its
political relations with other countries. Of passing
interest might also be mentioned that in the Miscelleanous
Papers are two files of THE COLORADO TIMES, a nespaper
published daily, except Sundays, at Denver, Colorado,
in the Japanese and English languages. The issues were
found to have been published in 1945.
Mexico City, D. F., Mexico.
American Embassy, June 26, 1946.
D.Y. Chang
É D.Coote.
Tien Yuen Chang
Edward Osborne Coote
First Secretary, Chinese Embassy
First Secretary, British Embassy
Vallhnith
Grigori Kasparov
Walter J. Linthicum
First Secretary, Embassy of the
Second Secretary, Embassy of
U.S.S.R.
the United States of America

Presente.
Muy respetables Señores:
Por medio de la presente, me permito dirigir a
Udes. el siguiente asunto y al mismo tiempo deceo
molestar una información de las direcciones y nombres
de la agencia a una fabrica que se venden en su pais.
Estando interegado por una maquinaria de fabricar
(Sepillos de dientes) según estoy pensando hacer un
pedido directo, pero mi idea es ponder una fabrica
pequeña es decir (Inaustrial familial) (sic) donde
trabajen mis hijos con poco capital, asi como quiero
una maquinaria sensilla, es esa toda mi molestia.
Muy agradecido por sus atenciones, me repito a
su afmo. atto. y S.S.

=== Page 536 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 10 to Memorandum of June 26, 1946,
from the American Embassy at Mexico City.
21
COPY
GRAL.HIGINIC ALVAREZ.
Leonardo de Vinci 67.
Mixcoac, D.F.
Mixcoac, D.F., Octubre 23 de 1939.
Excmo. Señor Ministro del Japón.
Legación del Japón.
México, D.F.
Excelentisimo Senor:
En el archivo de esa Honorable Legación, ahora al muy digno
cargo de S.E. existen antecedentes que comprueban mi sincera y
desinteresada estimación hacia el Pueblo Japonés y a su respe-
table Gobierno; por lo qué, ruego a S.E. reconocer que el con-
tenido de la presente, sólo puede inspirarlo, mi deseo por la
grandeza y prosperidad definitivas de ese Gran Imperio.
Hé seguido, detalle a detalle, el conrlicto con China, y estoy
seguro do que se ha prolongado, más por la ayuda proporcionada
a China por Inglaterra y Francia, que por la ayuda de Rusia.
Conozao también la forma altanera empleada por Inglaterra hacia
el Japón, en los incidentes ocurridos en las Conseciones británi-
cas en China, Por todos estos antecedentes y por ser quizá la
mejor oportunidad que se presente en la vida de las naciones, pa-
ra arrasar en forma definitiva el poderío inglés; me permito
respetuosamente sugerir, que es el momento preciso, para que el
Japon aprovechando la actual uerra europea, declare la guerra
a Inglaterra y a Francia, y controle de una vez y para siempre
la hegemonfa en el Asia, atacando a estas Potencias exclusiva-
mente en los mares asiáticos y en sus posesiones de Asia y de
Oceanfa; posesionándose en forma definitiva de Australia, de
la fracción inglesa de la península de Malaca, de la Base Naval
de Singapur y de la Indochina francesa; colaborando así con
Alemania, a librar ara siempre a la Humanidad, de la piratería
inglesa.
Tan o como estimo al Japón, estimo también a Alemania, y
por eso es cue me atrevo a o recer a S.., mi modesta colabora-
ción, para el caso de que S.. deseara aprovecharla, verbal y
extraoficialmente, cerca del Gobierno Alemán hasta lograr que di-
cho Gobierno ofreciera al Gobierno del Japón, su aprobación y apoyo
a las conquistas senaladas en mi párrafo anterior. Esta gestión
previa la aprobación verbal de .L. y de Vuestro Gobierno, la
harfa como idea original mía, ante el Gobierno Alemán, para no
herir suceptibilidades de ninguna de las dos Grandes Naciones.
Llevaré personalmente esta carta a esa Honorable Legación,
para no exponerla a ser violada, y si S.E. cree que merezca
tomarse en cuanta la sugestión que contiene, y acepta mi cola-
boración, a su llamado tendre el honor de acudir ante S.E.
Quizá podrá lograrse también por presión de Alemania, la
abstención absoluta por parte de Rusia en el actual conflicto
con China.
Acepte S.. las seguridades de mi sincero afecto y estima-
ción.
(firmado) GRAL. HIGINIO ALVAREZ

=== Page 537 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 11 to Memorandum of June 26, 1946,
from the American Embassy, Mexico City.
22
COPY
CONFIDENTIAL
Kult 6 - 1/E
(Translate from the Japanese: This letter
concerning the visa of the passport.
The German Secretary visited us and
reguested what this letter says. We
informed him it was impossible.)
La legación de Alemania saluda muy atentamente
a la Legación del Japón y se permite solicitar su
ayuda en el usunto siguiente:
Como ya se comunicó al Consulado General del
Japón con fecha 25 de este mes, Kult 6-1/E, el
ciudadano almán Alois Ecker, portador del pasaporte
Núm. 21/41, músico, intenta regresar, a causa de la
guerra en Europa, vía el Japón a Alemania. El citado
señor no está en ninguna manera en proporción con las
condiciones de Defensa y de la Marina y, según está
conocido a esta Legación, no ha sufrido castigo previo
en Alemania.
El mencionado señor intenta embarcarse en el
vapor "HIE MARU" que zarpará el 29 de enero de Man-
zanillo. En vista de cue el señor Ecker vino a México
procedente de los Estados Unidos de América con el fin
de escapar - por ser también ciudadano americano - al
servicio militar obligatorio de aquél país, teme que
pudieran surgir dificultades al anclar el vapor en
Los Angeles, si su nombre figura en la lista de pasa-
jeros:
Por lo expuesto la Legación de Alemania agrade-
cerfa especialmente a la Legación del Japón si pudiera
expedirse al senor Mcker una carta de recomendación al
capitán del vapor "HIE lkU" para que el senor Ecker
no aparezca en la lista de pasajeros. En caso que hubie-
se inconveniente para la expedición de tal carta, esta
Legación ruega atentamente de darle una recomendación,
también al senor canitán, indicándole cue preste al
señor Hcker toda ayuda posible.
En vista de la urgencia del caso, la Legación
agradece ía especialmente si el acunto pudiera ser
tratado desde luego. Con sus reiteradas gracias por
la fina atención que será dispensada a esta solicitud,
la Legación de Alemania aprovecha la oportunidad para
reiterar a la Legación del Japón las seguridades de su
muy atenta consideración.
México, D.F., a 27 de enero de 1941.
(Firmado) J. Fritz von Collenberg
Ministro de Alemania.

DEUTFCHE GEFANLTFCHAFT
México, D.F., 25 de enero de 1941
la. Córdoba 14
Legación de Alemania
México, D.F.
Kult 6 - 1 /E.
La Legación de Alemania saluda muy atentamente al
Consulado General del Japón y le ruega tenga a bien obor-
gar una visa de tránsito al ciudadano aleman
senor Alois E c k e r, portador del
pasaporte No. 21/41, expedido el 24
de enero de 1941 por esta Legación,
quien, a causa de la guerra en Europa, intenta regresar
vía el Japón a Alemania. El citado señor no está de
ninguna manera en proporción con las condiciones de
Defensa y de la Marina, y, según está conocido a esta
Legación, no ha surrido castigo previo en Alemania.
Anticipando sus más debidas gracias por la fina
atención que será dispensada a este asunto, la
Legación de Alemania aprovecha la oportunidad para
reiterar al Consulado General del Japón las seguri-
dades de su muy atenta consideración.
P.E.
(Firma) Firme ilegible.
Al Consulauo General del Japón,
Pre s ente.

Enclosure No. 12 to Memorandum of June 26, 1946,
from the American Embassy at Mexico City.
COPY
2
Monsieur le Chargé d'Affaires,
I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your
note of the l0th instant, in wnich, under instructions from
Peking, you were good enough to communicate to me the
decision of your Government respecting the abrogation of the
Sino-Japanese treaties and the notes of May 25, 1915. After
cuoting the statement of your Government, published immediately
on the conclusion of said treaties, the statement of the
Chinese Delegation at the Paris Peace Conference and the
contentions advanced by the Chinese Delegation at the Washing-
ton Conference, your note concludes that said treaties and
notes should now be cancelled in total except those stipula-
tions and reservations contained therein, which have already
been adjusted or which the Japanese Government have already
renounced or withdrawn.
The Japanese Government are unable to conceal from
themselves the sense of surprise and regret at the communi-
cation under acknowledgmont. The treaties concluded and
notes exchanged in 19l5 were formally signed by Japanese and
Chinese representatives, who were properly invested with full
powers by their respective governments, the treaties having
been, moreover, duly ratified by the respective heads of
state. The views of the Japanese Government on cancelling
these areements were declared by their delegates at the
Washington Conference.
The attempt on the part of your Government to abrogate
of its own accord treaties and notes wnich are perfectly valid,
will not only fail to contribute to the advancement of friend-
ship between our two countries, but should be regarded as
contrary to the accepted principles of international inter-
course. This government, accordingly, cannot in any way lend
themselves to the line of action now contemplated by your
Government. The Japanese Government have always had near
their neart the promotion of cordial relations between our two
nati ns, and they trust you will agree that their solicitude
in that direction has been abundantly proved in their dealings
with the Chinese Government by repeated acts of good will.
Furthermore, the Javanese Government have recently concluded
new arrangements with China on certain matters stipuluted in
said treaties and notes, and have also declared their decision
to waive rights secured to them under various other clauses
in the instruments in question, and to withdraw certain reser-
vations maae in them. In this situation they feel compelled
to declare that they rind absolutely nothing in the treaties
and notes nich is susceptible of further modification. It,
therefore, seems to the Japanese Government that there is no
occasion for entertaining in any way the proposals of your
Government respecting the discussion of questions incidental
to the restoration of Port rthur and Dairen, or conseguent
upon the abrogation of said treaties.

June 3, 1946
RE:
JAPANESE LEGATION FILES
Santiago, Chile
BYNOPSIS
There is set out an analysis by a British source of the translation
of the diary of KIYOSHI YAMAGATA, former Japanese Minister to Chile, covering
the entries from sugust 1, to iugust 31, 1942.
It has been previously reported that on November 6, 1945, United States
Ambassador to Chile, CLAuDE C. 3O/ER3, acting on behalf of the four Allied powers,
Great Britain, the UsS?, China and the United States, formally took over the
archives and property of the former Japanese Legation in Santiago, Chile. Upon
oxamining the archives, there was found the diary of Japanese Minister KIYosHI
YAMAGATA.
A Pritish source in Santiago, on analyzing the entries of the above
mentioned diary of KIYOS!I YAACATA, reported the following information on
the indiviluals mentioned therein:
ABURTO (OROSTECUI), RODPICO - Chilean journalist on the "DiariO Ilus-
trado," who was a leading propagandist for the Japanese in Chile, in the employ
of their Legation. He returned from Japan in September, 1942.
ALESSANDRI (PALMA), ARTURO - Liberal Farty. Ex-President of Chile,
now President of the Senate. During the war his attitude and contacts were
extreme rightist and pro-axis, although, in order to maintain his popularity
with the masses, he professes to be democratic. He strongly opposed Chile's
break vith axis powers and was in contact with espionage agents, ALBERTo
COLLELL(ROCA). connected with German and Japanese espionage, and with SOzAN
IYOSHI, Japanese espionage agent. ALEsSaNLRI was very friendly with the
Italian Ambassador, whom he kept informed on Chilean policy. AUGUSTO IGLESIAS,
pro-Japenese journalist, employed by the Japanese Legation, has, since his
return from Japan in September, 19L2, been editing ALSSaNDRI's autobiography.
The diary entries show that YAMAGATA made a special appeal to ALESSANDRI to
maintain Chile's neutrality, and that FRAlCISCo NAlYO, Japanese photographer,
was contacting ALESSAllDRI and reporting on the political situation to YAMAGATA.
BARY, CARLOS - Chilean Journalist on "El Chileno" who returned from
Japan in September, 192. He does not api ear to have been actively pro-Japanese,

Rio de Janeiro, Brazil
CONFIDINTIAL
July 2, 1946
MEMORANDUM FOR THE AMBASSADOR
Attention: Mr. H. V. Blocker
Re: ARCHIVES, JAPANESE EMBASSY
Reference is made to a recent communication received
by the Embassy from the State Department requesting further
information concerning certain items examined at the time
the Archives of the Japanese Embassy at Rio de Janeiro were
taken over by representatives of the Chinese, British and
American Embassies.
The "identification cards" mentioned in this dis-
patch consist of Police carteiras of identification and mem-
bership cards in such groups as the Jockey Club of Rio de
Janeiro and are considered to be of no intaligence value.
The "registration of Japanese Nationals" consists of
a group of registrations in Japanese writing. This group
was examined by the Counselor of the Chinese Embassy, Mr.
TAN PAU TUAN, who reads Japanese, and was deemed to be of
no value.
The "Passengers for Repatriation" consist of 1l2
filled-in forms giving limited biographical data on a group
of Japanese designated as repatriates. The names of these
repatriates were checked against our files without revealing
significant data of value. A copy of a list of these names
is attached. No dates of repatriation were given.
The "Secret Order of Blue Shirts" refers to a group
organized in China for action during the Sino-Japanese
war, against the Japanese. A photostatic copy of this
document is attached hereto for your information. No con-
nection with the Spanish Falange was apparent.
The "Cables of Japanese Nationals received and sent"
consisted of four copy-books containing handwritten copies
of cables dealing in protocol matters sent and received as
early as 1897 by the Japanese Legation (at that time) in
Petropolis, Brazil. The most recent of these cables was
found to be dated around l9l0.
Respectfully,
SAM J. PAPICH
Attachments
Legal Attaché
CONFIDENTIAL
